Global Complexification of Real Analytic Restricted Log-Exp-Analytic Functions
Andre Opris

Abstract




[columns=2, options=-s mystyle]

Abstract.

We show that a real analytic restricted log-exp-analytic function has a holomorphic extension which is again restricted log-exp-analytic. We also establish a parametric version of this result.

Introduction

This paper contributes to analysis in the framework of o-minimal structures. O-minimality is a concept from mathematical logic with connections and applications to geometry, analysis, number theory and other areas. Sets and functions definable in an o-minimal structure (i.e. ‘belonging to’) exhibit tame geometric, combinatorial and topological behaviour. This includes for example the cell decomposition theorem, the fact that every definable set is homeomorphic to a finite union of hypercubes and that every definable set has finitely many connected components which are again definable. We refer to the book of Van den Dries
[
11
]
for the general properties of o-minimal structures.

The major line of research was based on discovering o-minimal structures on the reals. For example the pure real field
\mathbb{R}
is o-minimal. The definable sets are exactly the semialgebraic sets since they are closed under projections by Alfred Tarski (see
[
36
]
; this was popularized by Abraham Seidenberg in
[
32
]
). But there are much bigger o-minimal structures on
\mathbb{R}
. Together with a theorem of Gabrielov Van den Dries could prove for example the remarkable result that
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
, the structure generated by all restricted analytic functions, is o-minimal (see
[
10
]
). The definable sets in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
are precisely the globally subanalytic ones. In
[
10
]
it is shown that
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
is polynomially bounded. So the global exponential function is not definable in there. Lou van den Dries and Chris Miller showed in
[
15
]
that we stay in the o-minimal context if we add the global exponential function (see also
[
13
]
). Then we obtain the o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
. This is one of the most important o-minimal structures, because a huge class of elementary functions like polynomial functions, the global arctangent function, the global logarithm, the global exponential function and hyperbolic functions are definable in there. They have crucial application in diophantine geometry (see for example
[
37
]
where definable functions with special diophantine properties and growth rates are investigated or
[
31
]
for the treatment of the André-Oort conjecture for
\mathbb{C}^{n}
from the viewpoint of o-minimality).

Kobi Peterzil and Sergei Stepanovich Starchenko designed the development of complex analysis within the o-minimal framework also in non-standard setting (see
[
30
]
). One obtains analogies of classical results as well as strong differences, due to the o-minimality assumption. For example the maximum principle, the open mapping theorem, the theorem of Liouville, the identity theorem and Riemann’s removeable theorem hold in this setting. However the key feature is that a definable holomorphic function in an o-minimal expansion of
\mathbb{R}
does not have an essential singularity. So the entire definable holomorphic functions are exactly the polynomials. Further results of Peterzil and Starchenko concern analytic geometry. For example in
[
29
]
they gave some stronger version of Chow’s theorem which states that a definable analytic subset of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is already an algebraic variety. All this theory can be formulated for an arbitrary real closed field
R
with algebraic closure
K=R[\sqrt{-1}]
. But to describe such features in this non-standard setting it is necessary to use ”topological analysis” instead of power series and integration.

So it would be desirable to understand definability in the complex setting outgoing from definability on the reals. A very interesting question in this context is the following.

Question

Are real analytic functions definable in an o-minimal structure
\mathcal{M}
on the reals reducts of definable holomorphic functions, i.e. does
\mathcal{M}
have global complexification?

To investigate this question one has to find a definable holomorphic extension for a given definable real analytic function. Of course finding a holomorphic extension is not difficult simply by power series expansion, but the crucial point is that this extension needs not to be definable. Tobias Kaiser affirmed this question for the o-minimal structures
\mathbb{R}
(see
[
18
]
) and
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
(see
[
17
]
). He could even establish parametric versions for this results.

However there is no general concept to solve such difficult problems and this can’t be generalized easily to arbitrary o-minimal structures on the reals. For example for
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
issues regarding global complexifixation are not solved at present. But there is the deep model theoretical fact that every definable function in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
is piecewise given by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
-terms (
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
is the language of ordered rings augmented by all restricted analytic functions, the global exponential and the global logarithm, see
[
13
]
). Kaiser used this fact to show in
[
17
]
that univariate real analytic functions which are definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
extend in a definably way to a holomorphic function. A quantitative version of this result was given by Kaiser and Speissegger in
[
21
]
(see also Wilkie in
[
37
]
). But especially for this big structure it would be desirable to have an analoguous result as in the globally subanalytic case, because there are many fields of application. A necessary condition is, of course, that the underlying function is real analytic. The idea is the same as in
[
17
]
for the globally subanalytic case: The first step is to use the preparation theorem of Lion and Rolin in order to construct an
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable holomorphic extension of a real analytic
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable function in one variable which is again
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable and the second step is to do a technical induction on the number of variables.

But there are strong differences between the structures
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
from the point of analysis: There are
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable functions which are
C^{\infty}
, but not real analytic (for example flat functions) or violate Tamm’s Theorem (see
[
14
]
,
[
19
]
or
[
28
]
for versions of Tamm’s Theorem) in contrast to globally subanalytic functions. So there is the natural question if there is a big non-trivial class of
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable functions which share its analytic properties with globally subanalytic ones, but behaves completely differently from the point of o-minimality. This applies for example for restricted log-exp-analytic functions which have been defined by Opris (see
[
17
]
). They are given piecewise by compositions of globally subanalytic functions, the global logarithm and exponentials of locally bounded functions. Since the global exponential function is restricted log-exp-analytic this class of functions is not polynomially bounded and generates the whole structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
.

Example

The function

g:\textnormal{}]0,1[^{2}\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto\arctan(\log(e^{1/t\cdot\log^{2}(1/x)}+\log(e^{e^{1/t}}+2))),

is restricted log-exp-analytic.

We will not answer the question above for all real analytic
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable functions, but for real analytic restricted log-exp-analytic functions. We obtain the following result.

Theorem A

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a definable open set. Let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
be a real analytic restricted log-exp-analytic function. Then there is a definable open
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
U\subset V
and a holomorphic restricted log-exp-analytic
F:V\to\mathbb{C}
such that
F|_{U}=f
(i.e. the real and imaginary part of
F
considered as real functions are restricted log-exp-analytic).

The idea for the proof of Theorem A is the same procedure as in the globally subanalytic case: Outgoing from a preparation theorem for restricted log-exp-analytic functions in
[
28
]
(see also Theorem C in
[
27
]
for a more general preparation theorem for
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable functions) we construct a holomorphic extension of a real analytic restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable which is again restricted log-exp-analytic and then we do a technical induction on the number of variables. This gives the following version of Theorem A for parameters.

Theorem B

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
be definable such that
X_{t}
is open for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto f(t,x)
, be restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
such that
f_{t}
is real analytic for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then there is a definable
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
with
X\subset Z
such that
Z_{t}
is open for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a function
F:Z\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto F(t,z),
which is restricted log-exp-analytic in
z
such that
F_{t}
is holomorphic for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
F|_{X}=f
(i.e. the real and imaginary part of
F
considered as real functions are restricted log-exp-analytic in
z
).

This paper is organized as follows. After a short introduction in o-minimality (compare with Section 1) we introduce in Section 2 global complexification and give examples. In Section 3 we pick up the definition of log-analytic functions, the exponential number and restricted log-exp-analytic functions from
[
28
]
. Section 4 is about a preparation theorem for definable functions from
[
27
]
and for restricted log-exp-analytic functions from
[
28
]
. Section 5 is devoted to the proofs of Theorem A and Theorem B.

Notations:

The empty sum is by definition
0
and the empty product is by definition
1
. By
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,...\}
we denote the set of natural numbers and by
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,...\}
the set of nonnegative integers. Let
\mathbb{R}^{*}:=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
we set
\mathbb{R}_{>a}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid x>a\}
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}
let
\lceil{x}\rceil
be the smallest integer which is not smaller than
x
and let
\textnormal{sign}(x)\in\{\pm 1\}
its sign if
x\neq 0
. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
we have
a<\infty
,
-\infty<a
and we set
a+\infty:=\infty
and
a-\infty:=-\infty
. Given a subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
we denote by
\overline{A}
its closure. By
\log_{k}
we denote the
k
-times iterated of the natural logarithm and by
\mathrm{exp}_{k}
the
k
-times iterated of the natural exponential (where
\log_{0}=\mathrm{exp}_{0}=\textnormal{id}
). Given
x=(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
, we define the box

Q^{n}(x,r):=\{(y_{1},...,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid|{y_{j}-x_{j}}|<r\textnormal{ for all }j\in\{1,...,n\}\}.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
we consider the following: For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
let
\textnormal{dist}(x,X):=\inf\{|{x-y}|\mid y\in X\}
. For two functions
f,g:X\to\mathbb{R}
we say that
f>g
if
f(x)>g(x)
for every
x\in X
. For a set
E
of positive real valued functions on
X
we set
\log(E):=\{\log(g)\mid g\in E\}
. For
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
C\subset X
and a set
E
of real valued functions on
X
we set
E|_{C}:=\{g|_{C}\mid g\in E\}
.

We set
\textnormal{sup}(\emptyset)=-\infty
and
\textnormal{inf}(\emptyset)=\infty
. For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
M(m\times n,\mathbb{Q})
the set of
m\times n
-matrices with rational entries. For
P\in M(m\times n,\mathbb{Q})

For
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}

X_{\neq 0}:=\{(x,y)\in X\mid y\neq 0\}
.

For
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

X_{t}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\mid(t,x)\in X\}

f:X\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto f(t,x),
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

f_{t}:X_{t}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto f(t,x)
.

We set
\mathbb{C}^{-}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
,
\mathbb{C}^{-}_{a}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\leq a}
and
\mathbb{C}^{+}_{a}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\geq a}
for
a\in\mathbb{R}
. We denote by
\mathrm{arg}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\to]-\pi,\pi]
the standard argument function and define
\log:\mathbb{C}^{-}\to\mathbb{C},z\mapsto\log(z):=\log(|{z}|)+i\textnormal{arg}(z)
, (i.e.
z
is mapped on the principal value of the complex logarithm) and
z^{q}:=e^{q\log(z)}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
and

. Let
z

\mathbb{C}

r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
c\in\mathbb{C}

A(c,r_{1},r_{2}):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid r_{1}<|{z-c}|<r_{2}\}.

For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}

B(c,r):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|{z-c}|<r\}.

Given
z=(z_{1},...,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
r>0
, we define the polydisc

D^{n}(z,r):=\{(w_{1},...,w_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid|{w_{j}-z_{j}}|<r\textnormal{ for all }j\in\{1,...,n\}\}.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be definable. A
curve
is a continuous mapping
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\to X
, a
subcurve
of a curve
\gamma:\textbf{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to X
is a curve
\hat{\gamma}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to X
of the following form: There is
\delta>1
such that
\hat{\gamma}(y)=\gamma(y/\delta)
for
y\in\textbf{}]0,1[
.

z

\mathbb{C}
. For

) . We set

\log^{*}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},z\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\log(z),&&|{z}|\in[1/2,3/2]\setminus\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0},\\
&\textnormal{if}&\\
0,&&\textnormal{else.}\end{array}\right.

Terminology from model theory

By
\mathcal{L}:=(\leq,+,\cdot,-,0,1)
we denote the language of ordered rings, by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}
the language of ordered rings augmented by symbols for all restricted analytic functions (compare with Definition 1.7 for the notion of a restricted analytic function), by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
the language
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}
augmented by symbols for the global exponential function and the global logarithm and by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(^{-1},(\sqrt[n]{...})_{n=2,3,...},\log)
the language
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}
augmented by a function symbol
-1
for taking reciprocals with respect to ”
\cdot
”, by function symbols
\sqrt[n]{...}
for taking the
n
’th root for
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 2
, and by a symbol for the global logarithm.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\in\{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}},\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(^{-1},(\sqrt[n]{...})_{n=2,3,...},\log),\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)\}
. An
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-term is inductively defined as follows.

(i)

0
and
1
(i.e. every constant symbol in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
) are
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-terms.

(ii)

Let
x_{1},x_{2},...
be the infinite list of variables in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. Then
x_{1},x_{2},...
are
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-terms.

(iii)

If
f
is an
m
-ary function symbol in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{1},...,t_{m}
are
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-terms then
f(t_{1},...,t_{m})
is an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-term.

Conventions

Definable means always
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable if not otherwise mentioned.

We identify
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
via
x+iy\mapsto(x,y)
. So
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
is definable if
Z
is definable considered as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

1
O-Minimal Structures on the Reals

1.1 Definition

A subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is called
semialgebraic
if there are
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and real polynomials
f_{i},g_{i,1},...,g_{i,k}\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},...,X_{n}]
for
1\leq i\leq l
such that

A=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i,1}(x)>0,...,g_{i,k}(x)>0\}.

A map is called semialgebraic if its graph is semialgebraic.

1.2 Definition

A subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is called
semianalytic
if for each
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
there are open neighbourhoods
U,V
of
a
with
\overline{U}\subset V
,
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and real analytic functions
f_{i},g_{i,1},...,g_{i,k}
on
V
for
1\leq i\leq l
, such that

A\cap U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}\{x\in U\mid f_{i}(x)=0,g_{i,1}(x)>0,...,g_{i,k}(x)>0\}.

A map is called semianalytic if its graph is semianalytic.

1.3 Definition

A subset
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is called
subanalytic
if for each
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
a
, some
p\geq n
and some bounded semianalytic set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
B\cap U=\pi_{n}(A)
where
\pi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},(x_{1},...,x_{p})\mapsto(x_{1},...,x_{n}),
is the projection on the first
n
coordinates.
A map is called subanalytic if its graph is subanalytic.

1.4 Remark

A semialgebraic set is semianalytic. A semianalytic set is subanalytic.

See
[
2
]
and
[
33
]
for geometrical descriptions of semianalytic resp. subanalytic sets and functions.

1.5 Definition

A subset
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is called
globally subanalytic
if it is subanalytic after applying the semialgebraic homeomorphism

\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\textnormal{}]-1,1[^{n},x_{i}\mapsto\frac{x_{i}}{\sqrt{1+x_{i}^{2}}},

for
i\in\{1,...,n\}
. A map is called globally subanalytic if its graph is globally subanalytic.

1.6 Example

(1)

A semialgebraic set is globally subanalytic.

(2)

The restriction of the global sine function on a compact interval is globally subanalytic.

1.7 Definition

A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is called
restricted analytic
if there is a real convergent power series
p
in
n
variables which converges absolutely on an open neighbourhood of
[-1,1]^{n}
such that

f(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p(x),&x\in[-1,1]^{n},\\
0,&\textnormal{ else.}\end{array}\right.

1.8 Definition

A
structure
on
\mathbb{R}
is axiomatically defined as follows. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
M_{n}
be a set of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\mathcal{M}:=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is a structure on
\mathbb{R}
if the following holds for all
m,n,p\in\mathbb{N}
.

(S1)

If
A,B\in\mathcal{M}^{n}
then
A\cup B
,
A\cap B
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A\in M_{n}
. (So
M_{n}
is a Boolean algebra of subsets of
M_{n}
.)

(S2)

If
A\in M_{n}
and
B\in M_{m}
then
A\times B\in M_{n+m}
.

(S3)

If
A\in M_{p}
and
p\geq n
then
\pi_{n}(A)\in M_{n}
where
\pi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},(x_{1},...,x_{p})\mapsto(x_{1},...,x_{n})
, denotes the projection on the first
n
coordinates.

(S4)

M_{n}
contains the semialgebraic subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The structure
\mathcal{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathbb{R}
is called
o-minimal
if additionally the following holds.

(O)

The sets in
M_{1}
are exactly the finite unions of intervals and points.

1.9 Definition

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a structure. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

a)

A subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is called
definable in
\mathcal{M}
if
A\in M_{n}
.

b)

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. A function
f:B\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is
definable in
\mathcal{M}
if its graph
\{(x,f(x))\mid x\in B\}
is definable in
\mathcal{M}
.

1.10 Remark

The global sine or cosine function is not definable in an o-minimal expansion of
\mathbb{R}
.

1.11 Example

(1)

The smallest o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
is given by the semialgebraic sets (see
[
36
]
and
[
32
]
). This is the pure real field and it is denoted by
\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
, the structure generated on the real field by the global exponential function
\mathrm{exp}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
(i.e. the smallest structure containing the semialgebraic sets and the graph of the exponential function), is o-minimal. (With a theorem of Khovanskii from
[
22
]
together with the result from
[
38
]
that the theory of
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
is model complete we obtain o-minimality.)

(3)

\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
, the structure generated on the real field by the restricted analytic functions, is o-minimal (see
[
10
]
together with Gabrielov’s theorem from
[
16
]
that the complement of a globally subanalytic function is again globally subanalytic; see also
[
25
]
, Section 1 for an analytic proof). The sets definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
are precisely the globally subanalytic ones (see
[
11
]
).

(4)

\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
, the structure generated by
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
, is o-minimal (see for example
[
13
]
for a model-theoretic proof respectively
[
25
]
, Section 2 for an analytic proof).

Especially the o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
is very important, because all elementary functions are definable in there. With the following fact we understand how the definable functions in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
look like.

1.12 Fact
(Van den Dries/Macintyre/Marker,
[
13
]
, Corollary 4.7)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
. Then there are
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
-terms
t_{1},...,t_{s}
such that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is
j\in\{1,...,s\}
with
f(x)=t_{j}(x)
. So
f
is
piecewise given by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
-terms
.

This means:

Definable functions are piecewise compositions of globally subanalytic functions, the global logarithm and the global exponential. So we call them also
log-exp-analytic
in some context.

Subsequently, we exhibit the tame geometric behaviour of o-minimal structures. An essential role in this context plays the cell decomposition theorem which states that we can decompose every definable set in finitely many disjoint subsets of a special form called cells. This concept is very helpful for technical proofs: The idea is to do calculation on every single cell at first and finally obtain the result by considering all of them.

For the rest of the section ”definable” means always ”definable in
\mathcal{M}
” if the underlying o-minimal structure
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathbb{R}
is clear from the context.

1.13 Definition

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
. By induction on
n
we define a
definable cell
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
as follows.

n=1
:
C
is either a singleton or an open interval.

n\to n+1
:
C
has one of the following form.

i)

C=\textnormal{graph}(f)
,

ii)

C=\{(t,x)\in B\times\mathbb{R}\mid f(t)<x<g(t)\}:=\textnormal{}]f,g[_{B}
,

iii)

C=\{(t,x)\in B\times\mathbb{R}\mid f(t)<x<\infty\}:=\textnormal{}]f,\infty[_{B}
,

iv)

C=\{(t,x)\in B\times\mathbb{R}\mid-\infty<x<f(x)\}:=\textnormal{}]-\infty,f[_{B}
,

v)

C=\{(t,x)\in B\times\mathbb{R}\mid-\infty<x<\infty\}:=\textnormal{}]-\infty,\infty[_{B}
,

where
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a definable cell called the
base
of
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f,g:B\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous definable functions with additionally
f<g
in ii).

Figure 1.1:
Different cell types in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
on the base
B

1.14 Definition

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be non-empty and definable. A finite partition
\mathcal{C}
of
A
into definable cells is called a
definable cell decomposition
\mathcal{C}
of
A
.

1.15 Theorem

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be definable. The following holds.

(1)

There is a definable cell decomposition
\mathcal{C}
of
A
.

(2)

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. If
f:A\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a definable function then the definable cell composition
\mathcal{C}
of
A
can be chosen in a way that
f|_{C}
is continuous for every
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof

We refer to Van den Dries (compare with
[
11
]
, Chapter 3).
\blacksquare

Since a definable cell is definably connected an immediate consequence from this theorem is that every definable set is the union of finitely many connected components and these are again definable. We refer to the book of Van den Dries in
[
11
]
or Coste in
[
9
]
for more on the general properties of o-minimal structures.

To close this section we pick up results on definability of parameterized integrals over the reals which will be important for our purposes in Section 5.2. The class of constructible functions which was introduced by Cluckers and Miller in
[
7
]
plays a major role in this context.

1.16 Definition

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a globally subanalytic set. A function
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
is called
constructible
if there are
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
l_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i\in\{1,...,k\}
and globally subanalytic functions
g_{i}:A\to\mathbb{R}
and
h_{ij}:A\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for
i\in\{1,...,k\}
and
j\in\{1,...,l_{i}\}
such that

f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}g_{i}(x)\prod_{j=1}^{l_{i}}\log(h_{ij}(x))

for every
x\in A
.

1.17 Remark

A globally subanalytic function is constructible.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
u
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
v
over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

1.18 Definition

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be globally subanalytic. We set

\textnormal{Fin}(f):=\bigl\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}|{f(u,v)}|dv<\infty\bigl\}.

1.19 Fact
(Comte/Lion/Rolin,
[
8
]
Theorem 1)

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be globally subanalytic. The following holds.

(1)

The set
\textnormal{Fin}(f)
is globally subanalytic.

(2)

The function

h:\textnormal{Fin}(f)\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f(u,v)dv,

is constructible.

1.20 Fact
(Cluckers/Miller,
[
6
]
Theorem 2.5)

The class of constructible functions is stable under parametric integration.

This means that the class of constructible functions is the smallest subclass of all definable functions in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
which contains all the globally subanalytic ones and is stable under parametric integration. Further and more deep theory on integration of constructible functions where loci of integrability and Lebesque classes of constructible functions are studied can be found in
[
5
]
and
[
6
]
.

However it is in general not possible to extend this result within
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
beyond the constructible setting as the following fact indicates.

1.21 Fact
(Van den Dries/Macintyre/Marker,
[
12
]
Theorem 5.11)

The function
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto\int_{0}^{u}e^{-v^{2}}dv,
is not definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
.

An important consequence of this fact is that even parameterized integrals of log-analytic functions are in general not definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
. (Compare with Definition 3.1 for the notion of a log-analytic function.)

1.22 Example

Consider

f:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},(u,v)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{1}{2v^{2}\sqrt{\log(v)}},&&1<v<u,\\
&\textnormal{if}&\\
0,&&\textnormal{else.}\end{array}\right.

We obtain for
1<u

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\log(u)}}e^{-v^{2}}dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\log(u)}\frac{e^{-v}}{2\sqrt{v}}dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{u}\frac{1}{2v^{2}\sqrt{\log(v)}}dv.

For
u\in\mathbb{R}
let

g(u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(u,v)dv.

We have
g(u)=0
for
u\leq 1
and

g(u)=\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\log(u)}}e^{-v^{2}}dv\leq\sqrt{\pi}

for
u>1
. So we see that
\textnormal{Fin}(f)=\mathbb{R}
. By Fact 1.21 we obtain that
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto\int_{0}^{u}e^{-v^{2}}dv,
is not definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
. Therefore
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(u,v)dv,
is also not definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
.

\blacksquare

Note that the antiderivative of a continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
can be considered as a special parameterized integral:

\int_{0}^{x}f(t)dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{f}(x,t)dt

where
\tilde{f}(x,t)=f(t)\mathbbm{1}_{[0,x]}(t)
. For an o-minimal structure
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathbb{R}
consider
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
, the Pfaffian closure of
\mathcal{M}
, which is an o-minimal expansion of
\mathcal{M}
. It goes beyond the scope of this article to give the exact definition. For more details we refer to
[
35
]
.

1.23 Fact
(Speissegger,
[
35
]
, Corollary on p.1)

Let
n=1
and
m=1
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be an o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
and let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be definable in
\mathcal{M}
. Then the function
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\int_{0}^{x}f(t)dt
, is definable in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
.

This shows that the parameterized integrals from Fact 1.21 and Example 1.22 are indeed definable in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}})
.
But questions about this matter regarding definability of such integrals in more than one variable are quite open. (See for example
[
20
]
for definability results and open questions regarding parameterized exponential integrals given by the Brownian motion on globally subanalytic sets.)

But outgoing from Fact 1.19 it is possible to obtain definability of parameterized integrals whose integrand is globally subanalytic and bounded in the variable of integration (compare with Definition 1.24). This result is very important for the technical proofs of Theorem A and Theorem B in Section 5.

For the rest of this section let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{K}
. Let
t
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
z
over
\mathbb{K}
and
\tau
over
\mathbb{R}
.

1.24 Definition

a)

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g:\pi(Z)\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
be definable. We call a function
f:Z\to\mathbb{C}

globally subanalytic in
z
with support function
g
respectively
constructible in
z
with support function
g
if there is a globally subanalytic respectively constructible function
F:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

f(t,z)=F(g(t),z)

for every
(t,z)\in Z
.

b)

We call a function
f:Z\to\mathbb{C}

globally subanalytic in
z
respectively
constructible in
z
if there is a globally subanalytic respectively constructible function
F:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{C}
and a definable
g:\pi(Z)\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
such that

f(t,z)=F(g(t),z)

for every
(t,z)\in Z
.

1.25 Remark

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
g:\pi(Z)\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
be definable. The following holds.

(1)

A globally subanalytic function resp. constructible function
Z\to\mathbb{C}
in
z
with support function
g
is definable.

(2)

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and let
G:\mathbb{C}^{q}\to\mathbb{C}
be a globally subanalytic function. Let
J_{1},...,J_{q}:Z\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic in
z
with support function
g
. Then

H:Z\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto G(J_{1}(t,z),...,J_{q}(t,z)),

is globally subanalytic in
z
with support function
g
.

Proof

(1): Clear.

(2): Let
K_{1},...,K_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic such that
J_{j}(t,z)=K_{j}(g(t),z)
for every
(t,z)\in Z
and
j\in\{1,...,q\}
. Let
w
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Then

F:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{C},(w,z)\mapsto G(K_{1}(w,z),...,K_{q}(w,z)),

is globally subanalytic. We have

F(g(t),z)=G(J_{1}(t,z),...,J_{q}(t,z))

for every
(t,z)\in Z
.
\blacksquare

1.26 Remark

Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
. Let
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be definable. Let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be with
a\leq b
. Let
Z=Y\times[a,b]
. Let
f:Z\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic in
\tau
with support function
g:Y\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Suppose that
f_{t}
is bounded for every
t\in Y
. Consider

F:Y\to\mathbb{C},t\mapsto\int_{a}^{b}f(t,\tau)d\tau.

Then
F
is well-defined and there is a constructible
H:\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
F(t)=H(g(t))
for every
t\in Y
.

Proof

Let
w
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Let
J:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C},(w,\tau)\mapsto J(w,\tau),
be globally subanalytic such that

f(t,\tau)=J(g(t),\tau)

for every
(t,\tau)\in Z
. Let
J=J_{1}+iJ_{2}
where
J_{1}
is the real part and
J_{2}
the imaginary part of
J
. For
j\in\{1,2\}
we consider

G_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},(w,\tau)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}J_{j}(w,\tau),&&\tau\in[a,b],\\
&\textnormal{if}&\\
0,&&\textnormal{else.}\end{array}\right.

Then
G_{j}
is globally subanalytic. Let
G:=G_{1}+iG_{2}
. By Fact 1.19(1) the set

\textnormal{Fin}(G):=\bigl\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{l}\mid\int_{\mathbb{R}}|{G_{j}(w,\tau)}|d\tau<\infty\textnormal{ for $j\in\{1,2\}$}\bigl\}

is globally subanalytic. We obtain
g(Y)\subset\textnormal{Fin}(G)
, because
f_{t}
is bounded for every
t\in Y
. With Fact 1.19(2) we obtain that the function
H:\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

H(w)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(w,\tau)d\tau=\int_{a}^{b}J(w,\tau)d\tau

if
w\in\textnormal{Fin}(G)
and
H(w)=0
otherwise is constructible. For
t\in Y
we have
F(t)=H(g(t))
.

\blacksquare

2
Complexification and Global

Complexification

In this section
\mathcal{M}
denotes a fixed o-minimal structure on
\mathbb{R}
and the expression ”definable” means always ”definable in
\mathcal{M}
” if not otherwise mentioned.

2.1 Definition

We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
complexification
if the following holds. Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
be open and let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a definable real analytic function. Then for every
x\in X
there is a definable open neighbourhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
of
x
, an open set
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{l}
with
U\subset V
, and a definable holomorphic function
F:V\to\mathbb{C}
such that
F|_{U}=f|_{U}
.

So
\mathcal{M}
has complexification if every definable real analytic function has locally a definable holomorphic extension.

2.2 Example

(1)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an o-minimal expansion of
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
(e.g.
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
). Then
\mathcal{M}
has complexification.

(2)

The o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
does not have complexification.

Proof

(1): This follows simply by power series expansion.

(2): We denote by
f
the real exponential function which is definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
. Consider

\mathrm{exp}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},x+iy\mapsto\mathrm{exp}(x)(\cos(y)+i\sin(y)),

which is holomorphic. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
and let
V
be an open ball in
\mathbb{C}
around
x
. By the identity theorem we see that
\mathrm{exp}|_{V}
is the unique holomorphic extension of
f|_{V\cap\mathbb{R}}
on
V
. But Bianconi showed in
[
1
]
that no restriction of the global sine function on a non-empty open interval is definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
.
\blacksquare

We mention that such questions are not answered completely. A field of open problems in this context are for example o-minimal structures which are generated by so called convergent Weiherstrass systems. We refer to
[
26
]
for the details.

2.3 Definition

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary global complexification
if the following holds. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
be open and let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a definable real analytic function. Then there is a definable open set
V
in
\mathbb{C}^{l}
with
X\subset V
and a definable holomorphic function
F:V\to\mathbb{C}
such that
F|_{X}=f
. Moreover we call
F
a
global complexification
of
f
.

So
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary global complexification for
l\in\mathbb{N}
if every definable real analytic function
F:X\to\mathbb{R}
where
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
is open has a definable holomorphic extension.

2.4 Remark

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\mathcal{M}
has
(l+1)
-ary global complexification then it has
l
-ary global complexification.

By Fact 1.12 every definable function in
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
is piecewise given by
\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{an}}(\mathrm{exp},\log)
-terms. A consequence is the following.

2.5 Fact
(Kaiser,
[
17
]
Theorem C)

The o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
has unary global complexification.

2.6 Definition

We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
global complexification
if
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary global complexification for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

So
\mathcal{M}
has global complexification if every definable real analytic function has a definable holomorphic extension.

2.7 Remark

If
\mathcal{M}
has global complexification then it has complexification. So
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{exp}}
does not have global complexification.

It is not known to us whether there is an o-minimal structure which has complexification but no global complexification. The problem is that there is no general concept how definable functions in o-minimal structures look like. But for the case that
\mathcal{M}
is the pure real field all definable real analytic functions are so called Nash functions. For this class of functions there are strong results like the implicit function theorem or the Artin-Mazur description (see
[
3
]
, Chapter 8). With such descriptions one gets the following.

2.8 Fact
(Kaiser,
[
18
]
Theorem B)

The pure real field
\mathbb{R}
has global complexification.

(See also Shiota in
[
34
]
, Chapter I.6.7.)

For the o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
the situation is much more complicated since much more functions than in
\mathbb{R}
are definable in there. Fortunately there are deep geometrical results for globally subanalytic functions. These are the preparation theorems of Lion and Rolin (see
[
25
]
) which give a nice representation of a globally subanalytic function in one variable and are precise enough to establish global complexification: The idea is to compute a suitable holomorphic extension of a real analytic globally subanalytic function in one variable at first and then do a non-trivial induction on the number of variables (see
[
17
]
). Therefore in
[
17
]
Tobias Kaiser set up the notion of parametric global complexification.

For
n,l\in\mathbb{N}
let
t
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
x
over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Let
z
range over
\mathbb{C}^{l}
.

2.9 Definition

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary parametric global complexification
if the following holds. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
be definable such that
X_{t}
is open for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto f(t,x),
be definable such that
f_{t}:X_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
is real analytic for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then there is a definable
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}
with
X\subset Z
such that
Z_{t}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{l}\mid(t,z)\in Z\}
is open for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a definable function
F:Z\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto F(t,z)
, such that
F_{t}
is holomorphic for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
F|_{X}=f
. We call
F:Z\to\mathbb{C}
a
parametric global complexification
of
f
.

2.10 Remark

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{M}
has
(l+1)
-ary parametric global complexification then it has
l
-ary parametric global complexification.

(2)

If
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary parametric global complexification then it has
l
-ary global complexification.

2.11 Definition

We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
parametric global complexification
if
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary parametric global complexification for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.12 Remark
(Kaiser,
[
17
]
Remark 1.13)

Also, if there is an open nonempty subset of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
that is contained in every
X_{t}
, there may be no
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}
such that there is an open and nonempty subset of
\mathbb{C}^{l}
that is contained in every
Z_{t}
as shown by the example

\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto 1/(t^{2}+x^{2}).

2.13 Remark

If
\mathcal{M}
has parametric global complexification then it has global complexification.

If one deals with parametric global complexification there is the problem to ”lift” the open property from the univariate case into the multivariate case since fiberwise open does not imply open. So we set up the concept of high parametric global complexification (see also
[
17
]
for the ideas).

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
let
u
range over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

2.14 Definition

(a)

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary high parametric global complexification
if the following holds. Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
be definable such that
X_{t}=\{(u,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}\mid(t,u,x)\in X\}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be definable such that
f_{t}:X_{t}\to\mathbb{R},(u,x)\mapsto f(t,u,x),
is real analytic for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then there is an
l
-ary parametric global complexification
F:Z\to\mathbb{C},(t,u,z)\mapsto F(t,u,z),
of
f
such that
Z_{t}=\{(u,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}\mid(t,u,z)\in Z\}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We call
F
a
high parametric global complexification of
f
with respect to
(u,x)
.

(b)

We say that
\mathcal{M}
has
high parametric global complexification
if
\mathcal{M}
has
l
-ary high parametric global complexification for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.15 Remark

If
\mathcal{M}
has high parametric global complexification then it has parametric global complexification and therefore global complexification.

2.16 Fact
(Kaiser,
[
17
]
Theorem 2.10)

The o-minimal structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
has high parametric global complexification. Therefore
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an}}
has parametric global complexification and global complexification.

For the rest of Section 2 let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
.

2.17 Example

Let
X:=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
and consider the definable function

f:X\to\mathbb{R},\textnormal{ }(u,x)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{x}{u}),&&u>0,\\
&\mbox{if}&\\
0,&&u\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

Let

Z:=\{(u,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\mid|{\textnormal{Im}(z)}|<\pi u\textnormal{ if }u>0\}.

Then the function

G:Z\to\mathbb{C},(u,z)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{z}{u}),&&u>0,\\
&\mbox{if}&\\
0,&&u\leq 0,\end{array}\right.

is a unary parametric global complexification of
f
.

2.18 Remark

Let
X
and
f
be as in Example 2.17. Let
Z\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}
be definable and let
F:Z\to\mathbb{C}
be a unary parametric global complexification of
f
. Then there is no
r>0
such that
]0,r[\textnormal{}\times(]0,r[+i]0,r[)\subset Z
, i.e.
F
is no unary high parametric global complexification with respect to
(u,x)
.

Proof

Note that
Z_{t}
is open for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume the contrary. Fix
r>0
such that

Q:=\textnormal{}]0,r[\times\textnormal{}(]0,r[\textnormal{}+i\textnormal{}]0,r[)\subset Z.

By the identity theorem we have for
(u,z)\in Z
that
F(u,z)=\mathrm{exp}(z/u)
if
u>0
and
F(u,z)=0
otherwise. Note that
Q
is definable, but
F|_{Q}
is not definable, because for every
(u,x+iy)\in Q
we have

F(u,x+iy)=\mathrm{exp}(x/u)(\cos(y/u)+i\sin(y/u))

and the global sine function (or cosine function) is not definable at infinity, a contradiction.
\blacksquare

Nevertheless it is absolutely possible that
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
has global complexification since the underlying function must be real analytic on its domain and
f
in Example 2.17 is not real analytic at zero.

3
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-Definable Functions

3.1
Log-Analytic Functions and the Exponential

Number

We fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a definable
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

3.1 Definition

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a function.

(a)

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. By induction on
r
we define that
f
is
log-analytic of order at most

r
.

Base case
: The function
f
is log-analytic of order at most
0
if there is a decomposition
\mathcal{C}
of
X
into finitely many definable sets such that for
C\in\mathcal{C}
there is a globally subanalytic function
F:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f|_{C}=F|_{C}
.

Inductive step
: The function
f
is log-analytic of order at most
r
if the following holds: There is a decomposition
\mathcal{C}
of
X
into finitely many definable sets such that for
C\in\mathcal{C}
there are
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, a globally subanalytic function
F:\mathbb{R}^{k+l}\to\mathbb{R}
, and log-analytic functions
g_{1},...,g_{k}:C\to\mathbb{R},h_{1},...,h_{l}:C\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
of order at most
r-1
such that

f|_{C}=F(g_{1},...,g_{k},\log(h_{1}),...,\log(h_{l})).

(b)

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We call
f

log-analytic of order

r
if
f
is log-analytic of order at most
r
but not of order at most
r-1
.

(c)

We call
f

log-analytic
if
f
is log-analytic of order
r
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

3.2 Definition

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a function. Let
E
be a set of positive definable functions on
X
.

(a)

By induction on
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define that
f
has
exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
.

Base Case
: The function
f
has exponential number at most
0
with respect to
E
if
f
is log-analytic.

Inductive Step
: The function
f
has exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
if the following holds: There are
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, functions
g_{1},...,g_{k}:X\to\mathbb{R}
and
h_{1},...,h_{l}:X\to\mathbb{R}
with exponential number at most
e-1
with respect to
E
and a log-analytic function
F:\mathbb{R}^{k+l}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

f=F(g_{1},...,g_{k},\mathrm{exp}(h_{1}),...,\mathrm{exp}(h_{l}))

and
\mathrm{exp}(h_{1}),...,\mathrm{exp}(h_{l})\in E
.

(b)

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We say that
f
has
exponential number
e
with respect to
E
if
f
has exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
but not at most
e-1
with respect to
E
.

(c)

We say that
f

can be constructed from
E
if there is
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
f
has exponential number
e
with respect to
E
.

3.3 Example

The function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\log(x^{2}+|{x}|+1),
is log-analytic. The function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\log(\mathrm{exp}(x+\mathrm{exp}(x))+1),
has exponential number at most
2
with respect to
E:=\{g_{1},g_{2}\}
where

g_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},x\mapsto\mathrm{exp}(x),\textnormal{ }g_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},x\mapsto\mathrm{exp}(x+\mathrm{exp}(x)).

3.4 Remark

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
E
be a set of positive definable functions on
X
.

(1)

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a function with exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
. Then
\mathrm{exp}(f)
has exponential number at most
e+1
with respect to
E\cup\{\mathrm{exp}(f)\}
.

(2)

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
f_{1},...,f_{s}:X\to\mathbb{R}
be functions with exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
and let
F:\mathbb{R}^{s}\to\mathbb{R}
be log-analytic. Then
F(f_{1},...,f_{s})
has exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
.

(See also
[
27
]
, Section 1 for Definition 3.1 to Remark 3.4 and also for some more facts and examples in this context.)

3.5 Remark

Let
X_{1},X_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be definable and disjoint. Let
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}
. For
j\in\{1,2\}
let
E_{j}
be a set of positive definable functions on
X_{j}
and
f_{j}:X_{j}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function. Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
f_{j}
has exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Let

E:=\{g\mid g:X\to\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ is a function with }g|_{X_{j}}\in E_{j}\textnormal{ for }j\in\{1,2\}\}.

Then

f:X\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{1}(x),&x\in X_{1},\\
f_{2}(x),&x\in X_{2},\end{array}\right.

has exponential number at most
e
with respect to
E
.

We need the concept of the exponential number also in the complex setting.

For the rest of Section 3.1 we fix a definable
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Let
z:=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
range over
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Let
x:=(x_{1},...,x_{m})
and
y:=(y_{1},...,y_{m})
range over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
z=x+iy
. We fix a function
F:Z\to\mathbb{C}
with
F(z)=u(x,y)+iv(x,y)
where
u
is the real and
v
the imaginary part of
F
considered as real functions.

3.6 Definition

We call
F
log-analytic if
u
and
v
are log-analytic.

3.7 Remark

The function
\mathbb{C}^{-}\to\mathbb{C},w\mapsto\log(w)
, is log-analytic.

Proof

Note that for every
w\in\mathbb{C}^{-}

\log(w)=\log(|{w}|)+i\mathrm{arg}(w).

We see that

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R},(u,v)\mapsto\log(\sqrt{u^{2}+v^{2}}),

is log-analytic and that

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R},(u,v)\mapsto\mathrm{arg}(u+iv),

is log-analytic (since it is globally subanalytic).
\blacksquare

3.8 Definition

Let
E
be a set of definable functions on
Z
without zeros. We say that
F
can be constructed from
E
if
u
and
v
can be constructed from

E^{\textnormal{Re}}:=\{\mathrm{exp}(\textnormal{Re}(g))\mid g\in\log(E)\}

where
\log(E):=\{\log(h)\mid h\in E\}
.

3.9 Remark

Let
E
be a set of positive real valued definable functions on
Z
. Assume that
F
takes only real values. Then Definition 3.8 coincides with Definition 3.2(c) if one considers
F
and every
g\in E
as a real function.

Proof

We have
F=u
,
v=0
and
E^{\textnormal{Re}}=\{\mathrm{exp}(\textnormal{Re}(g))\mid g\in\log(E)\}=E
since
\textnormal{Re}(g)=g
for every
g\in\log(E)
.
\blacksquare

We can formulate Remark 3.4 also for the complex setting.

3.10 Remark

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
E
be a set of definable functions on
Z
.

(1)

Assume that
\textnormal{Im}(F)
is bounded and that
F:Z\to\mathbb{C}
can be constructed from
E
. Then
\mathrm{exp}(F)
can be constructed from
D:=E\cup\{\mathrm{exp}(F)\}
.

(2)

Let
F_{1},...,F_{l}:Z\to\mathbb{C}
be functions which can be constructed from
E
and let
G:\mathbb{C}^{l}\to\mathbb{C}
be log-analytic. Then
G(F_{1},...,F_{l})
can be constructed from
E
.

Proof

(1): Note that
\mathrm{exp}(F)
is definable. Note that

\mathrm{exp}(F(x+iy))=\mathrm{exp}(u(x,y))(\cos(v(x,y))+i\sin(v(x,y)))

for
x+iy\in Z
. By Remark 3.4(1)
\mathrm{exp}(u)
can be constructed from
E^{\textnormal{Re}}\cup\{\mathrm{exp}(u)\}
. Let
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be such that
|{v}|\leq M
. Let
T\in\{\cos,\sin\}
. Then

T^{*}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T(x),&|{x}|\leq M,\\
0,&\textnormal{else},\end{array}\right.

is globally subanalytic. Therefore by Remark 3.4(2)

Z\mapsto\mathbb{R},x+iy\mapsto\mathrm{exp}(u(x,y))T^{*}(v(x,y)),

can be constructed from
E^{\textnormal{Re}}\cup\{\mathrm{exp}(u)\}=D^{\textnormal{Re}}
.

(2): Let
u_{j}
be the real part and
v_{j}
be the imaginary part of
F_{j}
considered as real functions, i.e.

F_{j}(x+iy)=u_{j}(x,y)+iv_{j}(x,y)

for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
and
x+iy\in Z
. Let
\tilde{u}
be the real part and
\tilde{v}
be the imaginary part of
G
considered as real functions. Then

G(F_{1},...,F_{l})=\tilde{u}(u_{1},...,u_{l},v_{1},...,v_{l})+i\tilde{v}(u_{1},...,u_{l},v_{1},...,v_{l}).

We are done with Remark 3.4(2).
\blacksquare

3.2
Restricted Log-Exp-Analytic Functions

The main results of this paper are formulated in the parametric setting. So we set up the concept of restricted log-exp-analytic functions in single variables.

For Section 3.2 let
t
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
x
over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We fix definable sets
C,X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
C\subset X
. Suppose that
X_{t}
is open for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

3.11 Definition

Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function.

(a)

We call
f

locally bounded in
x
with reference set
X
if the following holds. For
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
w\in X_{t}
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
w
in
X_{t}
such that
U\cap C_{t}=\emptyset
or
f_{t}|_{U\cap C_{t}}
is bounded.

(b)

Suppose that
C_{t}
is open for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We call
f

locally bounded in
x
if
f
is locally bounded in
x
with reference set
C
.

3.12 Remark

The set of locally bounded functions in
x
with reference set
X
on
C
is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with respect to pointwise addition and multiplication.

3.13 Remark

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\subset X
be disjoint definable sets such that
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
. Let
g_{j}:C_{j}\to\mathbb{R}
be locally bounded in
x
with reference set
X
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Then

g:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{1}(t,x),&(t,x)\in C_{1},\\
g_{2}(t,x),&(t,x)\in C_{2},\end{array}\right.

is locally bounded in
x
with reference set
X
.

3.14 Definition

Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function.

(a)

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We say that
f
is
restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
of order (at most)
e
with reference set
X
if
f
has exponential number (at most)
e
with respect to a set
E
of positive definable functions on
C
such that every
g\in\log(E)
is locally bounded in
x
with reference set
X
.

(b)

We say that
f
is
restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
with reference set
X
if
f
can be constructed from a set
E
of positive definable functions on
C
such that every
g\in\log(E)
is locally bounded in
x
with reference set
X
.

3.15 Remark

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. For
j\in\{1,...,l\}
let
f_{j}:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function which is restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
with reference set
X
. Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}
be log-analytic. Then

C\to\mathbb{R},y\mapsto F(f_{1}(y),...,f_{l}(y)),

is restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
with reference set
X
.

3.16 Definition

A function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
is called
restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
if
f
is restricted log-exp-analytic in
x
with reference set
X
.

3.17 Definition

Let
X
be open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. A function
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
is called
restricted log-exp-analytic
if
f
is restricted log-exp-analytic in
(t,x)
with reference set
X
.

(See also
[
28
]
, Section 2 for Definition 3.11 to Definition 3.17.)

3.18 Example

The function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\log(\mathrm{exp}(x+\mathrm{exp}(x))+1),
is restricted log-exp-analytic (of order at most
2
). The function

h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{-1/x},&x>0,\\
0,&\textnormal{ else, }\end{array}\right.

is not restricted log-exp-analytic since one has
h\sim e^{-1/x}
as
x\searrow 0
, but the function
h|_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
is

So we see that the class of restricted log-exp-analytic functions is a proper subclass of the class of all definable functions. Since the global logarithm function is log-analytic, but not globally subanalytic and the global exponential function is restricted log-exp-analytic, but not log-analytic we obtain the following chain.

\textbf{globally subanalytic}\subsetneq\textbf{log-analytic}\subsetneq\textbf{restricted log-exp-analytic}

\subsetneq\textbf{log-exp-analytic}=\textbf{definable}.

Finally we describe briefly the class of restricted log-exp-analytic functions in the complex setting.

For the rest of Section 3.2 let
z:=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
range over
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Let
x:=(x_{1},...,x_{m})
and
y:=(y_{1},...,y_{m})
range over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
z=x+iy
.

3.19 Definition

Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
be definable and let
F:D\to\mathbb{C}
be a function with
F(z)=u(x,y)+iv(x,y)
where
u
is the real and
v
is the imaginary part of
F
considered as real functions.

(a)

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
be open and definable with
D\subset Z
. We say that
F
is
restricted log-exp-analytic with reference set
Z
if
u
and
v
are restricted log-exp-analytic with reference set
Z
considering
Z
as a real set.

(b)

Assume that
D
is open in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. We say that
F
is
restricted log-exp-analytic
if
F
is restricted log-exp-analytic with reference set
D
.

For the next definition let
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
w
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
, let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
be a definable set and let
F:D\to\mathbb{C}
be a function with
F(t,w,z)=u(t,w,x,y)+iv(t,w,x,y)
where
u
is the real part and
v
is the imaginary part of
F
considered as real functions.

3.20 Definition

(a)

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
be definable with
D\subset Z
such that
Z_{t}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say that
f
is
restricted log-exp-analytic in
(w,z)
with reference set
Z
if
u
and
v
are restricted log-exp-analytic in
(w,x,y)
with reference set
Z
considering
Z
as a real set.

(b)

Suppose that
D_{t}
is open for every
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say that
f
is
restricted log-exp-analytic in
(w,z)
if
f
is restricted log-exp-analytic in
(w,z)
with reference set
D
.

4
A Preparation Theorem for Restricted

Log-Exp-Analytic Functions

In this section we give a preparation theorem for
\mathbb{R}_{\textnormal{an,exp}}
-definable functions from
[
27
]
. For its formulation technical tools like logarithmic scales,
C
-heirs and
C
-nice functions are needed (compare also with
[
27
]
). Then we give a preparation theorem for restricted log-exp-analytic functions from
[
28
]
.

For Section 4 let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
x
over
\mathbb{R}
. We fix a definable set
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

4.1 Definition

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A tuple
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},...,y_{r})
of functions on
C
is called an
r
-logarithmic scale
on
C
with
center

\Theta=(\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r})
if the following holds:

(a)

y_{j}>0
or
y_{j}<0
for every
j\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(b)

\Theta_{j}
is a definable function on
\pi(C)
for every
j\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(c)

For
(t,x)\in C
we have
y_{0}(t,x)=x-\Theta_{0}(t)
and
y_{j}(t,x)=\log(|{y_{j-1}(t,x)}|)-\Theta_{j}(t)
for every
j\in\{1,...,r\}
.

(d)

We have
\Theta_{0}=0
or there is
\epsilon_{0}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that
0<|{y_{0}(t,x)}|<\epsilon_{0}|{x}|
for all
(t,x)\in C
. For
j\in\{1,...,r\}
the following holds: We have
\Theta_{j}=0
or there is
\epsilon_{j}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that
0<|{y_{j}(t,x)}|<\epsilon_{j}|{\log(|{y_{j-1}(t,x)}|)}|
for all
(t,x)\in C
.

4.2 Definition

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},...,y_{r})
be a logarithmic scale on
C
.

(1)

For
q:=(q_{0},...,q_{r})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
and
(t,x)\in C
we write

|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes q}:=\prod_{j=0}^{r}|{y_{j}(t,x)}|^{q_{j}}.

(2)

We set
\textrm{sign}(\mathcal{Y}):=\sigma:=(\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{r})\in\{-1,1\}^{r+1}
which defines the
sign
of
\mathcal{Y}
.

4.3 Example

Let
n=1
and consider

C:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid t\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{},\textnormal{}\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-t-1/t}<x<\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1/t}\}.

Let
\Theta_{0}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\tfrac{1}{1+t}
,
\Theta_{1}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto-\tfrac{1}{t}
and
\Theta_{2}=0
. Let
y_{0}:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto x-\Theta_{0}(t)
, and inductively for
j\in\{1,2\}
let
y_{j}:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto\log(|{y_{j-1}(t,x)}|)-\Theta_{j}(t)
. Then
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
is a
2
-logarithmic scale with center
(\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2})
.

Proof

Since
\Theta_{0}<C
we see that
y_{0}>0
. So we have

y_{1}(t,x)=\log(y_{0}(t,x))-\Theta_{1}(t)

for
(t,x)\in C
. We have
\Theta_{0}+e^{\Theta_{1}}>C
and therefore
y_{1}<0
since
y_{1}
is strictly monotone increasing in
x
. This gives

y_{2}(t,x)=\log(-y_{1}(t,x))-\Theta_{2}(t)

for
(t,x)\in C
. We have
\Theta_{0}+e^{\Theta_{1}-1}<C
and therefore
y_{2}<0
and
\sigma_{2}=-1
. An easy calculation shows that
x-\Theta_{0}(t)<\epsilon_{0}x
and that

|{\log(x-\Theta_{0}(t))-\Theta_{1}(t)}|<\epsilon_{1}|{\log(x-\Theta_{0}(t))}|

for
(t,x)\in C
where
\epsilon_{0}:=1/2
and
\epsilon_{1}:=1/2
. So one sees that
(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
is a
2
-logarithmic scale with center
(\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2})
. We have
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,-1,-1)\in\{-1,1\}^{3}
.
\blacksquare

4.4 Definition

We call
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}

C
-heir
if there is
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, an
l
-logarithmic scale
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
with center
(\hat{\Theta}_{0},...,\hat{\Theta}_{l})
on
C
, and
j\in\{1,...,l\}
such that
g=\mathrm{exp}(\hat{\Theta}_{j})
.

4.5 Definition

We call
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}

C
-nice
if there is a set
E
of
C
-heirs such that
g
can be constructed from
E
.

Note that the class of log-analytic functions on
\pi(C)
is a proper subclass of the class of
C
-nice functions. (See
[
27
]
, Section 2.3 for further properties and examples for
C
-heirs and
C
-nice functions.)

4.6 Definition

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function. We say that
g
is
r
-log-analytically prepared in
x
with center
\Theta
if

g(t,x)=a(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes q}u(t,x)

for all
(t,x)\in C
where
a
is a definable function on
\pi(C)
which vanishes identically or has no zero,
\mathcal{Y}=(y_{0},...,y_{r})
is an
r
-logarithmic scale with center
\Theta
on
C
,
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
and the following holds for
u
. There is
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u=v\circ\phi
where
v
is a power series which converges on an open neighbourhood of
[-1,1]^{s}
with
v([-1,1]^{s})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\phi:=(\phi_{1},...,\phi_{s}):C\to[-1,1]^{s}
is a function of the form

\phi_{j}(t,x):=b_{j}(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes p_{j}}

for
j\in\{1,...,s\}
and
(t,x)\in C
where
b_{j}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
is definable for
j\in\{1,...,s\}
and
p_{j}:=(p_{j0},...,p_{jr})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
.
We call
a

coefficient
and
b:=(b_{1},...,b_{s})
a tuple of
base functions
for
f
. An
LA-preparing tuple
for
f
is then

\mathcal{J}:=(r,\mathcal{Y},a,q,s,v,b,P)

where

P:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}p_{10}&\cdot&\cdot&p_{1r}\\
\cdot&&&\cdot\\
\cdot&&&\cdot\\
p_{s0}&\cdot&\cdot&p_{sr}\\
\end{array}\right)\in M\big(s\times(r+1),\mathbb{Q}).

The following preparation theorem for a log-analytic function could be established in
[
27
]
.

4.7 Fact
(Opris,
[
27
]
Theorem A)

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be definable. Let
f_{1},....,f_{m}:X\to\mathbb{R}
be log-analytic functions of order at most
r
. Then there is a definable cell decomposition
\mathcal{D}
of
X_{\neq 0}
such that
f_{1}|_{D},...,f_{m}|_{D}
are
r
-log-analytically prepared in
x
with
D
-nice coefficient,
D
-nice base functions and common
D
-nice center for
D\in\mathcal{D}
.

So one sees that the coefficient, center and base functions in such a preparation are compositions of log-analytic functions and
C
-heirs. This
C
-heirs are in general not log-analytic as the following example shows.

4.8 Example
(Opris,
[
27
]
Example 2.37)

Consider the definable cell

C:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid 0<t<1,\textnormal{}\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-2/t+2e^{-1/t}}<x<\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1/t}\}.

There it is shown that
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto e^{-1/t},
is a
C
-heir. So the function

f:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto e^{-1/t}x,

is
0
-log-analytically prepared in
x
with center
0
with a
C
-nice coefficient which is not log-analytic.

4.9 Definition

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
. Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R}
be a function. Let
E
be a set of positive definable functions on
C
.

By induction on
e
we define that
f
is
log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared
in
x
with center
\Theta
with respect to
E
. To this preparation we assign a
preparing tuple
for
f
.

e=-1
: We say that
f
is log-exp-analytically
(-1,r)
-prepared in
x
with center
\Theta
with respect to
E
if
f
is the zero function. A preparing tuple for
f
is
(0)
.

e-1\to e
: We say that
f
is log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
with center
\Theta
with respect to
E
if

f(t,x)=a(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes q}\mathrm{exp}(c(t,x))u(t,x)

for every
(t,x)\in C
where
a:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
is
C
-nice,
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},...,y_{r})
is an
r
-logarithmic scale with
C
-nice center
\Theta
,
q=(q_{0},...,q_{r})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
,
\mathrm{exp}(c)\in E
where
c
is log-exp-analytically
(e-1,r)
-prepared in
x
with center
\Theta
with respect to
E
and the following holds for
u
. There is
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
u=v\circ\phi
where
\phi:=(\phi_{1},...,\phi_{s}):C\to[-1,1]^{s}
with

\phi_{j}(t,x)=b_{j}(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes p_{j}}\mathrm{exp}(d_{j}(t,x))

for
j\in\{1,...,s\}
where
p_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
,
b_{j}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
is
C
-nice,
\mathrm{exp}(d_{j})\in E
,
d_{j}:C\to\mathbb{R}
is log-exp-analytically
(e-1,r)
-prepared in
x
with center
\Theta
with respect to
E
and
v
is a power series which converges absolutely on an open neighbourhood of
[-1,1]^{s}
with
v([-1,1]^{s})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Theorem B above is formulated in the parametric setting. So we give a preparation theorem for restricted log-exp-analytic functions in single variables (here in the last
m+1
variables for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
).

For the rest of Section 4 let
m,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be with
n=l+m
. Let
w:=(w_{1},...,w_{l})
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
and
u:=(u_{1},...,u_{m})
over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}
be a definable set with
C\subset X
. Assume that
X_{w}
is open for every
w\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
.

4.10 Definition

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We call
f:C\to\mathbb{R},(w,u,x)\mapsto f(w,u,x),

(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
if
f
is log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
with respect to a set
E
of positive definable functions such that every
g\in\log(E)
is locally bounded in
(u,x)
with reference set
X
.

4.11 Example

Let
C=\textnormal{}]0,1[^{2}
and
X=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. The function

C\to\mathbb{R},(u,x)\mapsto u\cdot e^{x\cdot\log(x)},

is
(1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically
(1,1)
-prepared.

4.12 Proposition
(Opris,
[
28
]
Proposition 2.12)

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
. Let
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
be a restricted log-exp-analytic function in
(u,x)
of order at most
e
. Then there is
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and a definable cell decomposition
\mathcal{D}
of
X_{\neq 0}
such that
f|_{D}
is
(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
for every
D\in\mathcal{D}
.

5
Proof of our Main Results

This section is devoted to the proofs of our main results. Theorem A is an immediate consequence of Theorem B. So we focus on the proof of the latter. The procedure is roughly the following.

The first step is to construct a definable holomorphic extension of a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable on a single cell
C
. Then we have to glue the single extensions together in order to obtain a unary high parametric global complexification, an extension which is big enough to enable the induction on the number of variables and to construct a parametric global complexification in more than one variable.

The hard point here is the univariate case. For this we need a rather sophisticated set-up. Of course it is not difficult to construct a definable holomorphic extension of a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable, but the challenge is to construct a unary high parametric global complexification. For this we need also some definability results on integration (see also Kaiser in
[
17
]
for the ideas in the globally subanalytic setting).

Since this procedure is very long and technical we start our considerations with some important preliminary results.

5.1
Preparations - Some Technical Preliminary Results

For the whole Section 5.1 we set the following. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
t
range over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
x
over
\mathbb{R}
. Let
z
range over
\mathbb{C}
. We fix a non-empty definable cell
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{\neq 0}
and an
r
-logarithmic scale
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},...,y_{r})
on
C
with center
\Theta:=(\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r})
. We fix
\textrm{sign}(\mathcal{Y}):=\sigma:=(\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{r})\in\{-1,1\}^{r+1}
.

Furthermore we say that a function
F:Y\to\mathbb{C}
where
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}
is definable (such that
Y_{t}
is open for
t\in\pi(Y)
) is continuous (holomorphic) in
z
if
F_{t}
is continuous (holomophic) for every
t\in\pi(Y)
.

5.1.1
Geometrical Properties and Holomorphic Extensions of Logarithmic Scales in One Variable

The goal for this subsection is to compute a definable holomorphic extension of the logarithmic scale
\mathcal{Y}
in
x
: Therefore one has to extend
y_{0},...,y_{r}
in a natural way to functions
g_{0},...,g_{r}
beyond the cell.

For Section 5.1.1 we set
D_{0}:=\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}
and

g_{0}:D_{0}\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto x-\Theta_{0}(t).

For
l\in\{1,...,r\}
we set inductively

D_{l}:=\{(t,x)\in D_{l-1}\mid\sigma_{l-1}g_{l-1}(t,x)>0\}

and

g_{l}:D_{l}\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto\log(\sigma_{l-1}g_{l-1}(t,x))-\Theta_{l}(t).

Set

D:=\{(t,x)\in D_{r}\mid\sigma_{r}g_{r}(t,x)>0\}.

5.1 Remark

We have
C\subset D\subset D_{r}\subset...\subset D_{1}
and for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
we have that
g_{l}|_{C}=y_{l}
, and
(g_{l})_{t}:(D_{l})_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
is injective and real analytic for every
t\in\pi(C)
.

5.2 Definition

Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. Let
\mu_{0,l}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\Theta_{l}(t),
and inductively for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
we set

\mu_{j,l}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\Theta_{l-j}(t)+\sigma_{l-j}e^{\mu_{j-1,l}(t)}.

Set
\mu_{l}:=\mu_{l,l}
.

5.3 Remark

Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. The functions
\mu_{l}
and
\mu_{j,l}
for
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
are definable.

It turns out that the graph of
\mu_{l}
is disjoint from
C
and exactly the zero set of
g_{l}
for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. Additionally the sign of
\mathcal{Y}
defines uniquely the position of the graph of
\mu_{l}
with respect to
C
and with respect to the graph of
\mu_{j}
for
j\neq l
. (Compare with Proposition 5.4 and Corollary 5.5. The functions
\mu_{j,l}
for
0\leq j<l
are needed for technical reasons in this context.)

5.4 Proposition

Let
t\in\pi(C)
. Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
and
j\in\{0,...,l\}
. We have
(t,\mu_{l}(t))\in D_{j}
and

g_{j}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\sigma_{j}e^{\mu_{l-j-1,l}(t)}

if
j<l
.

Proof

That
(t,\mu_{l}(t))\in D_{0}
is clear. So suppose that
l>0
. We show the statement by induction on
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

j=0
: With Definition 5.2 we obtain

g_{0}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\mu_{l}(t)-\Theta_{0}(t)=\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{l-1,l}(t)}.

j-1\to j
:
By the inductive hypothesis
(t,\mu_{l}(t))\in D_{j-1}
and

g_{j-1}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\sigma_{j-1}e^{\mu_{l-j,l}(t)}.

So we see that
(t,\mu_{l}(t))\in D_{j}
. If
j<l
we obtain

\displaystyle g_{j}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\log(\sigma_{j-1}g_{j-1}(t,\mu_{l}(t)))-\Theta_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{l-j,l}(t)-\Theta_{j}(t)=\sigma_{j}e^{\mu_{l-j-1,l}(t)}.

\blacksquare

5.5 Corollary

For
l\in\{0,...,r\}
and
t\in\pi(C)
we have
g_{l}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=0
and

Z(g_{l}):=\{(t,x)\in D_{l}\mid g_{l}(t,x)=0\}=\{(t,x)\in D_{l}\mid x=\mu_{l}(t)\}.

Proof

For
l=0
this is clear. So assume
l>0
. With Proposition 5.4 we obtain
(t,\mu_{l}(t))\in D_{l}
,

g_{l-1}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\sigma_{l-1}e^{\Theta_{l}(t)},

and therefore

g_{l}(t,\mu_{l}(t))=\log(\sigma_{l-1}g_{l-1}(t,\mu_{l}(t)))-\Theta_{l}(t)=0

for every
t\in\pi(C)
. With Remark 5.1 we are done.
\blacksquare

For the next proposition we say the following.

For a function
f:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
we say that
f
is (strictly) monotone increasing in
x
if
f_{t}
is (strictly) monotone increasing for all
t\in\pi(C)
and (strictly) monotone decreasing in
x
if
f_{t}
is (strictly) monotone decreasing for all
t\in\pi(C)
respectively.

5.6 Proposition

Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. The following properties hold.

(1)

The function
g_{l}
is strictly monotone in
x
. It is strictly monotone increasing in
x
if and only if
\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\sigma_{j}=1
.

(2)

Either
\mu_{l}<C
or
\mu_{l}>C
. We have
\mu_{l}<C
if and only if
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=1
.

(3)

Let
k\in\{l+1,...,r\}
.
Either
\mu_{l}<\mu_{k}
or
\mu_{k}<\mu_{l}
. we have
\mu_{l}<\mu_{k}
if and only if
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=1
.

Proof

(1): We do an induction on
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. For
l=0
it is clear.

l-1\to l
: We assume
\sigma_{l-1}=1
. The case ”
\sigma_{l-1}=-1
” is handled completely similar. Then
g_{l}
is strictly monotone increasing in
x
if and only if
g_{l-1}
is strictly monotone increasing in
x
. By the inductive hypothesis we have that
g_{l-1}
is strictly monotone increasing in
x
if and only if
\prod_{j=0}^{l-2}\sigma_{j}=1
. This gives the result.

(2): Because
g_{l}|_{C}=y_{l}
we obtain with Definition 4.1
g_{l}|_{C}>0
or
g_{l}|_{C}<0
. We assume
g_{l}|_{C}>0
, i.e.
\sigma_{l}=1
. The case ”
g_{l}|_{C}<0
” is handled completely similar. By (1) we have that
g_{l}
is either strictly monotone increasing in
x
or strictly monotone decreasing in
x
. If the former holds then
\mu_{l}<C
by Corollary 5.5. Additionally
\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\sigma_{j}=1
by (1) and therefore
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=1
. If the latter holds then
\mu_{l}>C
by Corollary 5.5. Additionally
\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\sigma_{j}=-1
by
(1)
and therefore
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=-1
.

(3): With Proposition 5.4 we obtain

g_{l}(t,\mu_{k}(t))=\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{k-l-1,k}(t)}

for every
t\in\pi(C)
. We assume that
g_{l}
is strictly monotone increasing in
x
. The case ”
g_{l}
is strictly monotone decreasing” is handled completely similar. By (1)
\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\sigma_{j}=1
. If
\sigma_{l}=1
then
g_{l}(t,\mu_{k}(t))=e^{\mu_{k-l-1,k}(t)}>0
for every
t\in\pi(C)
and consequently
\mu_{l}<\mu_{k}
by Corollary 5.5. If
\sigma_{l}=-1
then
g_{l}(t,\mu_{k}(t))=-e^{\mu_{k-l-1,k}(t)}<0
for every
t\in\pi(C)
and consequently
\mu_{k}<\mu_{l}
by Corollary 5.5.

\blacksquare

So one sees immediately that if
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,...,1)
the graphs of all zeros
\mu_{0},...,\mu_{r}
are below the cell and if
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(-1,1,...,1)
the graphs of all zeros
\mu_{0},...,\mu_{r}
are above the cell.

Figure 5.1:
The set
D
and the functions
\mu_{j}
for a
2
-logarithmic scale
\mathcal{Y}
on
C
with continuous center if
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,1,1)
on the left and
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(-1,1,1)
on the right.

If
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})
has another value than
(1,1,...,1)
or
(-1,1,...,1)
then there is a zero whose graph is above the cell and a zero whose graph is below the cell. To identify these zeros whose graphs are ”closest” to the cell also in this case we introduce the following.

5.7 Definition

We define the
change index

k^{\textnormal{ch}}
for
\mathcal{Y}
as follows: If there is
l\in\{0,...,r\}
with
\sigma_{l}=-1
set

k^{\textnormal{ch}}:=\max\{l\in\{0,...,r\}\mid\sigma_{l}=-1\}-1.

Otherwise set
k^{\textnormal{ch}}:=-2
.

5.8 Remark

If
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=-2
then
\sigma_{0}=...=\sigma_{r}=1
. If
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=-1
then
\sigma_{0}=-1
and
\sigma_{1}=...=\sigma_{r}=1
.

One sees that if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}<0
the graphs of all zeros are either below the cell (if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=-2
which means
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,...,1)
) or above the cell (if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=-1
which means
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(-1,1,...,1)
).

5.9 Remark

Let
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
. The following holds.

(1)

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
and
k<0
then

D=\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid x>\mu_{r}(t)\}.

(2)

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
and
k<0
then

D=\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid x<\mu_{r}(t)\}.

(3)

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
and
k\geq 0
then

D=\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid\mu_{r}(t)<x<\mu_{k}(t)\}.

(4)

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
and
k\geq 0
then

D=\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid\mu_{k}(t)<x<\mu_{r}(t)\}.

Proof

We show property (3). The rest is proven similarly. Note that
\prod_{j=0}^{k}\sigma_{j}=-1
,
\mu_{r}<C<\mu_{k}
and
\sigma_{\kappa}g_{\kappa}(t,x)=\sigma_{\kappa}y_{\kappa}(t,x)>0
for
(t,x)\in C
and
\kappa\in\{k,r\}
.

Let
(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}
be with
\mu_{r}(t)<x<\mu_{k}(t)
. If
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=1
then
\mu_{l}<\mu_{r}
if
l\neq r
by Proposition 5.6(3). If
\prod_{j=0}^{l}\sigma_{j}=-1
then
\mu_{k}<\mu_{l}
if
l\neq k
(since then
l<k
). So we obtain with the intermediate value theorem, Definition 4.1 and Corollary 5.5 that
\sigma_{j}g_{j}(t,x)>0
for every
j\in\{0,...,r\}
since
C\subset D
and
(g_{j})_{t}
is continuous . Therefore
(t,x)\in D
.

Let
(t,x)\in D
. Then we have
\sigma_{\kappa}g_{\kappa}(t,x)>0
for
\kappa\in\{k,r\}
. Again with Proposition 5.6(2), Corollary 5.5, the continuity of
g_{\kappa}
in
x
and the monotony property of
g_{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in\{k,r\}
we obtain the result.
\blacksquare

So one sees that if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
, i.e.
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})\notin\{(1,...,1),(-1,1,...,1)\}
, that the graphs of
\mu_{r}
and
\mu_{k^{\textnormal{ch}}}
are ”closest” to the cell (one from above and the other from below).

Figure 5.2:
The set
D
and the functions
\mu_{j}
for a
2
-logarithmic scale
\mathcal{Y}
on
C
with
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,-1,-1)
and continuous center. Here we have
r=2
and
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=1
.

5.10 Example

Let
n=1
and consider

C:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid t\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{},\textnormal{}\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-t-1/t}<x<\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1/t}\}.

Let
\Theta_{0}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\tfrac{1}{1+t}
,
\Theta_{1}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto-\tfrac{1}{t}
and
\Theta_{2}=0
. Let
y_{0}:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto x-\Theta_{0}(t)
, and inductively for
j\in\{1,2\}
let
y_{j}:C\to\mathbb{R},(t,x)\mapsto\log(|{y_{j-1}(t,x)}|)-\Theta_{j}(t)
. Then
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
is a
2
-logarithmic scale with center
(\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2})
and
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y}):=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=(1,-1,-1)\in\{-1,1\}^{3}
. Therefore
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=1
,
\mu_{0}=\Theta_{0}
,
\mu_{1}=\Theta_{0}+e^{\Theta_{1}}
,
\mu_{2}=\Theta_{0}+e^{\Theta_{1}-1}
,
\mu_{0,1}=\Theta_{1}
,
\mu_{0,2}=0
and
\mu_{1,2}=\Theta_{1}-1
.

Proof

Compare with the proof of Example 4.3.
\blacksquare

Now we are able to compute the desired definable holomorphic extension of
\mathcal{Y}
in
x
. So we merge into the complex setting.

5.11 Definition

(a)

Consider
H_{0}:=\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
and

z_{0}:H_{0}\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto z-\Theta_{0}(t).

Inductively for
l\in\{1,...,r\}
we set

H_{l}:=\{(t,z)\in H_{l-1}\mid\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}\}

and

z_{l}:H_{l}\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\log(\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{l}(t).

(b)

Set

H:=\{(t,z)\in H_{r}\mid\sigma_{r}z_{r}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}\}.

5.12 Remark

The following holds for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(1)

We have
C\subset D_{l}\subset H_{l}
,
H_{l}\subset H_{l-1}
if
l>0
and
(H_{l})_{t}
is open for every
t\in\pi(C)
. Additionally
z_{l}
is well-defined and holomorphic in
z
.

(2)

We have
z_{l}|_{D_{l}}=g_{l}
and
z_{l}|_{C}=y_{l}
.

(3)

Let
E
be a set of positive definable functions such that
\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r}
can be constructed from
E
. Then
z_{l}
can be constructed from
E
.

Proof

(1): This follows by an easy induction on
l
and the fact that the complex logarithm function is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}^{-}
.

(2): This follows immediately by an easy induction on
l
and from Definition 5.11(a).

(3): We do an induction on
l
. We have
z_{0}(t,z)=z-\Theta_{0}(t)
for every
(t,z)\in H_{0}
and are done with Remark 3.10(2).

l-1\to l
: We have
z_{l}(t,z)=\log(\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{l}(t)
for every
(t,z)\in H_{l}
and are done with the inductive hypothesis and Remark 3.10(2).
\blacksquare

We often consider
z_{l}
as a function on
H
for every
l\in\{0,...,r\}
.

Remark 5.12 shows that
z_{l}
is a complex extension of
y_{l}
in the last variable
z
which is holomorphic in
z
.

5.13 Remark

Let
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
.

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
and
k<0
then

H=\{(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\mid z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{r}(t)}\}.

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
and
k<0
then

H=\{(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\mid z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mu_{r}(t)}\}.

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
and
k\geq 0
then

H=\{(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\mid z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{r}(t)}\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mu_{k}(t)}\}.

If
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
and
k\geq 0
then

H=\{(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\mid z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mu_{r}(t)}\cap\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{k}(t)}\}.

Proof

We have

H=D\cup(\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}),

because for
(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
we obtain
\sigma_{l}z_{l}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
for every
l\in\{0,...,r\}
by the definition of the complex logarithm function. We are done with Remark 5.12(2) and Remark 5.9.
\blacksquare

5.14 Definition

Let
q:=(q_{0},...,q_{r})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. We set

(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}:H\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\prod_{j=0}^{r}(\sigma_{j}z_{j}(t,z))^{q_{j}}.

5.15 Remark

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. The following holds.

(1)

H_{t}
is open for every
t\in\pi(C)
and
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
is well-defined and holomorphic in
z
. In particular

(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}|_{C}=|{\mathcal{Y}}|^{\otimes q}.

(2)

Let
E
be a set of positive definable functions such that
\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r}
can be constructed from
E
. Then
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
can be constructed from
E
.

Proof

Note that
\sigma_{l}z_{l}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
for every
(t,z)\in H
and
l\in\{0,...,r\}
and that for
p\in\mathbb{Q}
the function
\mathbb{C}^{-}\to\mathbb{C},z\mapsto z^{p},
