is holomorphic and globally subanalytic. So property (1) follows from Remark 5.12(1) and Remark 5.12(2). Property (2) follows immediately with Remark 5.12(3) and Remark 3.10(2).
\blacksquare

For the rest of Section 5.1.1 we fix
\kappa=r
or
\kappa\in\{k^{\textnormal{ch}},r\}
if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
.

For technical reasons it is very useful to describe
z_{l}
as a function of
z-\mu_{\kappa}
. This helps enormously to answer questions on definability of parameterized integrals where complex logarithmic scales are involved.

5.16 Definition

Let
H_{0,\kappa}^{*}:=\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}:H_{0,\kappa}^{*}\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto z-\mu_{\kappa}(t),
and inductively for
l\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
let

H_{l,\kappa}^{*}:=\{(t,z)\in H_{l-1,\kappa}^{*}\mid 1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\in\mathbb{C}^{-}\}

and

\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}:H_{l,\kappa}^{*}\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\log\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl).

Set
\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa,\kappa}
.

5.17 Remark

Let
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. The function
\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}
is well-defined and definable. Furthermore we have
(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t))\in H_{l,\kappa}^{*}
and
\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t))=0
for
t\in\pi(C)
.

5.18 Proposition

Let
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. We have
H_{l,\kappa}^{*}=H_{l}
and if
l\neq\kappa
then

z_{l}(t,z)=\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}

for
(t,z)\in H_{l}
.

Proof

By definition of
H_{0,\kappa}^{*}
we have
H_{0,\kappa}^{*}=H_{0}
. If
\kappa=0
we are done. So assume that
\kappa>0
. We do an induction on
l
. For
(t,z)\in H_{0,\kappa}^{*}
we obtain

\displaystyle z_{0}(t,z)=z-\Theta_{0}-\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t)}+\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)+\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t)}=\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t)}.

l-1\to l
: Note that
H_{l-1}=H^{*}_{l-1,\kappa}
. Let
(t,z)\in H_{l-1}
. We get with the inductive hypothesis (since
l-1\neq\kappa
)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}(z_{l-1}(t,z)-\sigma_{l-1}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)})}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}.

So we obtain
H_{l}=H_{l,\kappa}^{*}
. Suppose
l\neq\kappa
. Let
(t,z)\in H_{l}
. We obtain with the inductive hypothesis

\displaystyle z_{l}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{l-1}(\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{l-1}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}))-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)+e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)})-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)+\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)+\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)+\Theta_{l}(t)+\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}-\Theta_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}.

\blacksquare

5.19 Corollary

For
(t,z)\in H_{\kappa}=H_{\kappa,\kappa}^{*}
we have
z_{\kappa}(t,z)=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}(t,z)
.

Proof

Let
\kappa=0
. Then
\Theta_{\kappa}=\mu_{\kappa}
and therefore

z_{\kappa}(t,z)=z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}(t,z)

for
(t,z)\in H_{\kappa}
. So let
\kappa>0
. With Proposition 5.18 we have for
(t,z)\in H_{\kappa}

\displaystyle z_{\kappa}(t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{\kappa-1}z_{\kappa-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{\kappa-1}(\mathcal{P}_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{k-1}e^{\mu_{0,\kappa}(t)}))-\Theta_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{\kappa-1}(\mathcal{P}_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t,z)+\sigma_{k-1}e^{\Theta_{\kappa}(t)}))-\Theta_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\sigma_{\kappa-1}\mathcal{P}_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t,z)+e^{\Theta_{\kappa}(t)})-\Theta_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{\kappa-1}\mathcal{P}_{\kappa-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\Theta_{\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}(t,z).

\blacksquare

We always consider
\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}
as a function on
H
for
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
.

5.20 Example

Let
n=1
and consider

C:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid t\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{},\textnormal{}\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-t-1/t}<x<\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1/t}\}.

Let
\mathcal{Y}:=(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
and
\Theta:=(\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2})
be as in Example 5.10. We have

H=\{(t,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}\mid\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1-1/t}<z<\tfrac{1}{1+t}+e^{-1/t}\textnormal{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}\}.

For
(t,z)\in H
we have

z_{0}(t,z)=z-\Theta_{0}(t),\textnormal{}z_{1}(t,z)=\log(z_{0}(t,z))-\Theta_{1}(t),

z_{2}(t,z)=\log(-z_{1}(t,z))-\Theta_{2}(t)

and

\mathcal{P}_{0,1}(t,z)=z-\mu_{1}(t),\textnormal{ }\mathcal{P}_{1,1}(t,z)=\log(1+\frac{z-\mu_{1}(t)}{e^{\mu_{0,1}(t)}}),

\mathcal{P}_{0,2}(t,z)=z-\mu_{2}(t),\textnormal{ }\mathcal{P}_{1,2}(t,z)=\log\bigl(1+\frac{z-\mu_{2}(t)}{e^{\mu_{1,2}(t)}}\bigl),

\mathcal{P}_{2,2}(t,z)=\log\bigl(1-\frac{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{0,2}(t)}}\bigl)

where
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{0,1},\mu_{0,2}
and
\mu_{1,2}
are as in Example 5.10.

5.21 Remark

Let
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}
is holomorphic in
z
. For
(t,z)\in H
we have
(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
and
(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
if
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof

The holomorphy is clear. If
l=\kappa
we obtain with Corollary 5.19

\sigma_{\kappa}\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}(t,z)=\sigma_{\kappa}z_{\kappa}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}

and if
z\in\mathbb{R}
then
(t,z)\in D
(compare with Remark 5.13 and Remark 5.9) and therefore
\sigma_{\kappa}\mathcal{P}_{\kappa}(t,z)=\sigma_{\kappa}g_{\kappa}(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by construction of
D
and Remark 5.12(2). So assume
l\neq\kappa
. With Proposition 5.18

\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)=\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)z_{l}(t,z)-\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}.

Since
z_{l}(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
for
(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
we may assume
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore
(t,z)\in D
. We show

\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

by induction on
l\in\{0,...,\kappa-1\}
. For
l=0
we obtain

\bigl(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}(t,z)=\bigl(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)(z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)).

The following is easy to see with Remark 5.9: If
\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}=1
then
\mu_{\kappa}(t)<z
and therefore
z-\mu_{r}(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. (If
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}<0
then
\kappa=r
and therefore
\mu_{r}<z
if
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
by (1) in Remark 5.9. If
k\geq 0
and
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
then
\kappa=r
(since
\prod_{j=0}^{k}\sigma_{j}=-1
) and therefore
\mu_{r}<z
by (3) in Remark 5.9. If
k\geq 0
and
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
then
\kappa=k
(since
\prod_{j=0}^{k}\sigma_{j}=1
) and therefore
\mu_{k}<z
by (4) in Remark 5.9.) In a completely similar way one sees that if
\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}=-1
then
z<\mu_{\kappa}(t)
and therefore
\mu_{\kappa}(t)-z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

l-1\to l:
With the inductive hypothesis and Proposition 5.18 we obtain

\bigl(\prod_{j=l-1}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)=\bigl(\prod_{j=l-1}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)z_{l-1}(t,z)-\bigl(\prod_{j=l-1}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\sigma_{l-1}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

Assume
\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}=1
. The case ”
\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}=-1
” is handled completely similar. We obtain

\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z)>e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}

and by taking logarithm

\log(\sigma_{l-1}z_{l-1}(t,z))>\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t).

This gives

z_{l}(t,z)>\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)-\Theta_{l}(t)=\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}

and therefore

\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)=\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)z_{l}(t,z)-\bigl(\prod_{j=l}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

This finishes the proof.
\blacksquare

5.1.2
Asymptotical Behaviour of Logarithmic Scales Under Taking Definable Curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}

With the monotonicity theorem in o-minimal structures (see
[
11
]
, Section 3) we see that for a definable function
g:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}
its limit
\lim_{x\searrow 0}g(x)
exists in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
.
So we are able to investigate the asymptotical behaviour of
\Theta
,
\mathcal{Z}
and
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
under taking a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
. This describes also the behaviour of these functions on the boundary of
H
.

Outgoing from Definition 4.1 we establish a nice ”boundedness condition” for
\Theta
on so called fat cells. This condition is essential for everything that follows and implies for example that
\Theta
is locally bounded if
C
is open.

5.22 Definition

For
t\in\pi(C)
we define the
length of
C
with respect to
x
as
L_{C}(t):=\sup(C_{t})-\inf(C_{t})
.

By the definition of a definable cell we have either
L_{C}(t)>0
for every
t\in\pi(C)
or
L_{C}(t)=0
for every
t\in\pi(C)
(see Definition 1.13).

5.23 Definition

We say that
C
is
fat
with respect to
x
if
L_{C}(t)>0
for every
t\in\pi(C)
(i.e.
C_{t}
is an open interval for every
t\in\pi(C)
).

For the rest of Section 5.1 we assume that
C
is fat with respect to
x
.

5.24 Notation

Let
y
range over
\mathbb{R}
. For a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
with
\gamma:=(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{n+1})
we set
\gamma_{t}:=(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{n})
for the first
n
real components,
\gamma_{z}:=\gamma_{n+1}
and if
\gamma_{n+1}(y)\in\mathbb{R}
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we also write
\gamma_{x}
instead of
\gamma_{z}
.

5.25 Remark

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve. Then
\lim_{y\searrow 0}L_{C}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
.

We get all the desired information about the behaviour of
\Theta
under taking a definable curve from Definition 4.1(d). But the underlying curve has to fulfill a special property.

5.26 Definition

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve. We say that
\gamma
is
compatible with
C
if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}
,
\lim_{y\searrow 0}L_{C}(\gamma_{t}(y))>0
and there is
\epsilon\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that

\{\inf(C_{\gamma_{t}(y)})\mid y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[\}

is bounded from above and

\{\sup(C_{\gamma_{t}(y)})\mid y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[\}

is bounded from below.

Note that a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C
is compatible with
C
if and only if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}L_{C}(\gamma_{t}(y))>0
and
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Figure 5.3:
The curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{3}
are compatible with
C
while the curves
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{4}
aren’t. (For
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
the arrows show
\gamma_{j}(y)
for decreasing
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
.)

5.27 Remark

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
. The following holds.

(1)

There is a continuous definable function
\tilde{\gamma}_{x}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}:=(\gamma_{t},\tilde{\gamma}_{x})
is a definable curve in
C
compatible with
C
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\textnormal{dist}(\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y),\mathbb{R}\setminus C_{\gamma_{t}(y)})>0.

So for every
l\in\{0,...,r\}
we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)-\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|>0.

(2)

Let
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y):=(t_{0},x_{0})
where
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\textnormal{dist}(\textnormal{Re}(\gamma_{z}(y)),C_{\gamma_{t}(y)})=0.

Then there is a definable function
\hat{\gamma}_{x}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\hat{\gamma}:=(\gamma_{t},\hat{\gamma}_{x})
is a definable curve in
C
compatible with
C
and we have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{t}(y),\hat{\gamma}_{x}(y))=(t_{0},x_{0})
.

Proof

We assume that

C=\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid f_{1}(t)<x<f_{2}(t)\}

where
f_{1},f_{2}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
are definable and continuous with
f_{1}<f_{2}
. The other cases are handled completely similar (compare with Definition 1.13 iii) - v) for the other types of a definable fat cell). Note that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(f_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))-f_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y)))>0
.

(1): Since
\gamma
is compatible with
C
we find continuous definable functions
h_{1}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
and
h_{2}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
with
h_{1}<h_{2}
such that

\{(t,x)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}\mid h_{1}(t)<x<h_{2}(t)\}\subset C,

\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and
\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))-h_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
(Choose
h_{1}=f_{1}
and
h_{2}=\min\{f_{1}+1,f_{2}\}
if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))\neq-\infty
,
h_{1}=\max\{f_{1},f_{2}-1\}
and
h_{2}=f_{2}
if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))\neq\infty
and otherwise
h_{1}=\max\{f_{1},0\}
and
h_{2}=\min\{f_{2},1\}
.)

Consider

\tilde{\gamma}_{x}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R},y\mapsto\frac{h_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))+h_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{2}.

By o-minimality we obtain that
]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C,y\mapsto(\gamma_{t}(y),\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)),
is a definable curve compatible with
C
with

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\textnormal{dist}(\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y),\mathbb{R}\setminus C_{\gamma_{t}(y)})\geq\lim_{y\searrow 0}\frac{h_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))-h_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{2}>0.

We see that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|>0
since
\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y))\notin C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
and
j\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(2): There is
0<\epsilon<1
such that
\textnormal{Re}(\gamma_{z}(y))\in C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[
or
\textnormal{Re}(\gamma_{z}(y))\notin C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[
. Assume the latter. The former is handled completely similar.

Suppose that
\textnormal{Re}(\gamma_{z}(y))>C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[
. The case ”
\textnormal{Re}(\gamma_{z}(y))<C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,\epsilon[
” is handled completely similar. Note that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))=x_{0}
. Fix a continuous definable function
\hat{f}_{1}
with
f_{1}<\hat{f}_{1}<f_{2}
such that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\hat{f}_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider

\hat{\gamma}_{x}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R},y\mapsto f_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))+y(\hat{f}_{1}(\gamma_{t}(y))-f_{2}(\gamma_{t}(y))).

Note that
\hat{\gamma}_{x}(y)\in C_{\gamma_{t}(y)}
for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
. Since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\hat{\gamma}_{x}(y)=x_{0}
by construction we see that
\hat{\gamma}_{x}
does the job.
\blacksquare

Now we are able to give the desired boundedness condition for
\Theta
.

5.28 Proposition

Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
. Then the following holds.

(I_{l})

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\Theta_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
.

(II_{l})

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|=0
if and only if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0
. We have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|=\infty
if and only if there is
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
such that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0
.

Proof

Fix a definable curve
\tilde{\gamma}
which fulfills the properties from Remark 5.27(1). By o-minimality we have that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\Theta_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
. We show both statements by induction on
l
simultaneously.

l=0
: Assume
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\Theta_{0}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|=\infty
. So
\Theta_{0}\neq 0
. By Definition 4.1(d) there is
\epsilon_{0}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that

|{x-\Theta_{0}(t)}|<\epsilon_{0}|{x}|

for all
(t,x)\in C
. Since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)
exists we get

+\infty=\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\Bigl|1-\frac{\Theta_{0}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)}\Bigl|\leq\epsilon_{0}.

This is a contradiction. This gives
(I_{0})
. Since

z_{0}(\gamma_{z}(y))=\gamma_{z}(y)-\Theta_{0}(\gamma_{t}(y))=\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{0}(\gamma_{t}(y))

for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we obtain
(II_{0})
(since the second part in
(II_{0})
cannot enter).

l-1\to l
: Assume
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\Theta_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|=\infty
. So
\Theta_{l}\neq 0
. So by Definition 4.1(d) there is
\epsilon_{l}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that

|{\log(\sigma_{l-1}y_{l-1}(t,x))-\Theta_{l}(t)}|<\epsilon_{l}|{\log(\sigma_{l-1}y_{l-1}(t,x))}|

for all
(t,x)\in C
. Therefore

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\Bigl|1-\frac{\Theta_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{\log(\sigma_{l-1}y_{l-1}(\tilde{\gamma}(y)))}\Bigl|\leq\epsilon_{l}.

This gives
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\sigma_{l-1}y_{l-1}(\tilde{\gamma}(y))\in\{0,\infty\}
. With
(II_{l-1})
we find
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
such that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0.

But this is a contradiction to the choice of
\tilde{\gamma}
. We obtain
(I_{l})
.

We show
(II_{l})
. We have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|=\infty
if and only if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l-1}(\gamma(y))}|\in\{0,\infty\}
by
(I_{l})
if and only if there is
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
such that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0
by
(II_{l-1})
.
We see with
(I_{a})
for
a\in\{1,...,l\}
that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{l-j}(\gamma(y))=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\sigma_{l-j}e^{\mu_{j-1,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}

if and only if

\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{l-j-1}(\gamma(y))=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\sigma_{l-j-1}e^{\mu_{j,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}

by an easy induction on
j\in\{1,...,l-1\}
(and the continuity of the global exponential function and the global logarithm on the positive real line). So we obtain

\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{l}(\gamma(y))=0
if and only if
\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{l-1}(\gamma(y))=\sigma_{l-1}e^{\mu_{0,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}
by
(I_{l})
if and only if

\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{0}(\gamma(y))=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{l-1,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}

if and only if

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)=\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\Theta_{0}(\gamma_{t}(y))+\sigma_{0}e^{\mu_{l-1,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))})=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))

with
(I_{0})
.

\blacksquare

The boundedness condition for
\Theta
can be formulated also in the following way: If for
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
t_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\epsilon>0
and
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
L_{C}(t)>\epsilon
,
\inf(C_{t})<c_{1}
and
\sup(C_{t})>c_{2}
for every
t\in\pi(C)\cap U
then
\Theta_{j}
is bounded at
t_{0}
for every
j\in\{0,...,r\}
(i.e. there is
V\subset U
open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
t_{0}\in V
such that
\Theta_{j}|_{\pi(C)\cap V}
is bounded).

So we see also that
\Theta
is locally bounded if
C
is open. Consequently every
C
-nice function is restricted log-exp-analytic if
C
is open.

An immediate consequence of this boundedness condition is the following.

5.29 Corollary

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
. Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. The following holds.

(1)

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\mu_{j,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
for every
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

(2)

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

for every
j\in\{0,...,r\}
with
j\neq l
.

Proof

(1): This is clear with the continuity of the global exponential function,
(I_{j})
in Proposition 5.28 and the definition of
\mu_{j,l}
for
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

(2): Let
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
. With Definition 5.2 and
(I_{a})
in Proposition 5.28 for
a\in\{0,...,j-1\}
we obtain that

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l-a,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{j-a,j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|>0

if and only if

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l-a-1,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{j-a-1,j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|>0

by an easy induction on
a\in\{0,...,j-1\}
. So we have

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|>0

since

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l-j,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{0,j}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|=\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}e^{\mu_{l-j-1,l}(\gamma_{t}(y))}>0

with (1).
\blacksquare

The following example shows that it is in general necessary to look at definable curves which are compatible with
C
to obtain such a boundedness condition.

5.30 Example

(1)

Let
C
,
y_{0},y_{1}
and
\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1}
be as in Example 5.10. Note that
(y_{0},y_{1})
is a
1
-logarithmic scale with center
(\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1})
. Although the cell is bounded we have
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\Theta_{1}(t)=-\infty
and
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\mu_{1}(t)-\mu_{0}(t)=0
. But we have
\lim_{t\searrow 0}L_{C}(t)=0
.

(2)

Let

C:=\{(t,x)\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}\mathbb{R}\mid e^{1/t}<x-3e^{1/t}<e^{1/2+1/t}\}

and consider
\hat{\Theta}_{0}:=3e^{1/t}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{1}:=1/t
. A simple calculation shows that there is a logarithmic scale
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
on
C
with center
\hat{\Theta}:=(\hat{\Theta}_{0},\hat{\Theta}_{1})
. Although
\lim_{t\searrow 0}L_{C}(t)>0
we see
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\hat{\Theta}_{0}(t)=\infty
,
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\hat{\Theta}_{1}(t)=\infty
and
\lim_{t\searrow 0}|{\mu_{1}(t)-\mu_{0}(t)}|=\infty
. But we have
\lim_{t\searrow 0}\inf(C_{t})=\infty
.

Figure 5.4:
Possible
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
for
2
-logarithmic scales on two different cells: In the right case one has control on
\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and their distance since
C
is bounded and
L_{C}
is bounded by a positive number from below. In the left case it is possible that
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
respectively
\mu_{2}
are unbounded at
t_{0}
respectively
t_{1}
(since
\inf(C_{t})
is not bounded from above at
t_{0}
and
L_{C}
is not bounded by a positive number from below at
t_{1}
).

Outgoing from this boundedness condition we are able to look at the asymptotical behaviour of
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
under taking a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
compatible with
C
.

5.31 Corollary

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
. Then the following holds.

(1)

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. Let
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|\in\{0,\infty\}
. Then there is
l\in\{0,...,r\}
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{l}(\gamma_{t}(y))=0
.

(2)

Assume
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))=0
. Then

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(\gamma(y))=0

for every
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
.

Proof

(1): This is clear with
(II_{l})
in Proposition 5.28 for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(2): We do an induction on
l
. If
l=0
then this is clear with Definition 5.16.

l-1\to l
: By Definition 5.16 we obtain

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(\gamma(y))=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\log\bigl(1+\frac{\sigma_{l-1}\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(\gamma(y))}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))}}\bigl)=0

by the inductive hypothesis, Corollary 5.29(1) and the continuity of the global logarithm at
1
.
\blacksquare

5.32 Definition

Assume
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
. Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
with
(q_{k+1},...,q_{r})\neq 0
. Set

l_{\min}(q):=\textnormal{min}\{j\in\{k+1,...,r\}\mid q_{j}\neq 0\}.

5.33 Definition

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
be with
(q_{\kappa},...,q_{r})\neq 0
. Let
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
.

a)

Let
\kappa=r
. We call
q

r
-positive
if
q_{r}>0
. We call
q

r
-negative
if
q_{r}<0
.

b)

Let
\kappa=k\geq 0
. We call
q

k
-positive
if
q_{k}>0
or if
q_{k}=0
then
q_{l_{\min}}<0
. We call
q

k
-negative
if
q_{k}<0
or if
q_{k}=0
then
q_{l_{\min}}>0
.

Note that a
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
with
(q_{\kappa},...,q_{r})\neq 0
is either
\kappa
-positive or
\kappa
-negative.

5.34 Proposition

Let
q:=(q_{0},...,q_{r})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
. Suppose
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))=0
. Then the following holds.

(1)

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for
l<\kappa
.

(2)

Suppose
(q_{\kappa},...,q_{r})\neq 0
. If
q
is
\kappa
-positive then
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|=0
. If
q
is
\kappa
-negative then
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|=\infty
.

Proof

(1): Assume there is
l\in\{0,...,\kappa-1\}
such that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|\in\{0,\infty\}
. Then with
(II_{l})
in Proposition 5.28 we find
j\in\{0,...,l\}
such that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0
and therefore

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\mu_{j}(\gamma_{t}(y)))=0,

a contradiction to Corollary 5.29(2). We obtain the assertion with o-minimality.

(2): If
\kappa=r
we obtain with
(II)_{r}
in Proposition 5.28
\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{r}(\gamma(y))=0
. With
(1)
we are done. So let
0\leq k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
and suppose
\kappa=k
. Let

W:=\{(t,z)\in H\mid|{z_{k}(t,z)}|<1/\mathrm{exp}_{r-k}(1)\},

L_{1}:W\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\log(|{z_{k}(t,z)}|),

and for
l\in\{2,...,r-k\}

L_{l}:W\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\log_{l-1}(-\log(|{z_{k}(t,z)}|)).

Note that
L_{l}
is well-defined and definable for
l\in\{1,...,r-k\}
. By o-minimality and considering a suitable subcurve of
\gamma
if necessary we may assume that
\gamma(]0,1[)\subset W
(since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}z_{k}(\gamma(y))=0
by
(II_{k})
in Proposition 5.28). Note also that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}L_{1}(\gamma(y))=-\infty
and that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}L_{l}(\gamma(y))=\infty
for
l\in\{2,...,r-k\}
.

Claim

Let
l\in\{1,...,r-k\}
. There is a definable function
h_{l}:W\to\mathbb{C}
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{l}(\gamma(y))=0
such that

z_{k+l}(t,z)=L_{l}(t,z)(1+h_{l}(t,z))

for every
(t,z)\in W
.

Proof of the claim

We do an induction on
l
. Suppose
l=1
. We have for
(t,z)\in W

z_{k+1}(t,z)=\log(|{z_{k}(t,z)}|)\Bigl(1-\frac{\Theta_{k+1}(t)-i\mathrm{arg}(\sigma_{k}z_{k}(t,z))}{\log(|{z_{k}(t,z)}|)}\Bigl).

So choose

h_{1}:W\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto-\frac{\Theta_{k+1}(t)-i\mathrm{arg}(\sigma_{k}z_{k}(t,z))}{\log(|{z_{k}(t,z)}|)}.

We have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{1}(\gamma(y))=0
by
(I_{k+1})
and
(II_{k})
in Proposition 5.28.

l=2
: Note that
\sigma_{k+1}=-1
. We have for
(t,z)\in W

z_{k+2}(t,z)=\log(\sigma_{k+1}L_{1}(t,z)(1+h_{1}(t,z)))-\Theta_{k+2}(t)

=\log(-L_{1}(t,z))\bigl(1+\frac{\log(1+h_{1}(t,z))-\Theta_{k+2}(t)}{\log(-L_{1}(t,z))}\bigl).

Choose

h_{2}:W\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\frac{\log(1+h_{1}(t,z))-\Theta_{k+2}(t)}{\log(-L_{1}(t,z))}.

Then
h_{2}
is definable and by
(I_{k+2})
in Proposition 5.28 we get
\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{2}(\gamma(y))=0
.

l-1\to l
: We may assume that
l>2
. Note that
\sigma_{k+l-1}=1
. With the inductive hypothesis we obtain

z_{k+l}(t,z)=\log(\sigma_{k+l-1}L_{l-1}(t,z)(1+h_{l-1}(t,z)))-\Theta_{k+l}(t)

=\log(L_{l-1}(t,z))\bigl(1+\frac{\log(1+h_{l-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{k+l}(t)}{\log(L_{l-1}(t,z))}\bigl)

for every
(t,z)\in W
. Choose

h_{l}:H\to\mathbb{C},(t,z)\mapsto\frac{\log(1+h_{l-1}(t,z))-\Theta_{k+l}(t)}{\log(L_{l-1}(t,z))}.

Then
h_{l}
is definable and by
(I_{k+l})
in Proposition 5.28 we get
\lim_{y\searrow 0}h_{l}(\gamma(y))=0
.

\blacksquare_{\textnormal{Claim}}

Let
h_{l}
and
L_{l}
be as in the claim for
l\in\{1,...,r-k\}
. Then we obtain for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[

|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|=d(\gamma(y))|{z_{k}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{k}}\prod_{l=1}^{r-k}L_{l}(\gamma(y))^{q_{k+l}}(1+h_{l}(\gamma(y)))^{q_{k+l}}

where

d(\gamma(y)):=\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}|{z_{l}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{l}}.

Note that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}d(\gamma(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by (1). Since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{k}(\gamma(y))}|=0
we see with the growth properties of the iterated logarithm that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{k}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{k}}\prod_{l=1}^{r-k}|{L_{l}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{k+l}}=0

if
q
is
k
-positive and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{z_{k}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{k}}\prod_{l=1}^{r-k}|{L_{l}(\gamma(y))}|^{q_{k+l}}=\infty

if
q
is
k
-negative.
So one sees that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|=0
if
q
is
k
-positive and
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(\gamma(y))}|=\infty
if
q
is
k
-negative. This proves Proposition 5.34.
\blacksquare

Proposition 5.34 gives the following growth property for the derivative of
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
.

5.35 Corollary

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
with
(q_{\kappa},...,q_{r})\neq 0
. Let
j_{\kappa}(q):=\min\{j\in\{\kappa,...,r\}\mid q_{j}\neq 0\}
and

q_{\kappa,\textnormal{diff}}:=(0,...,0,q_{\kappa}-1,...,q_{j_{\kappa}(q)}-1,q_{j_{\kappa}(q)+1},...,q_{r}).

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H
be a definable curve compatible with
C
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))=0
. Then

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\Bigl|\frac{(\frac{d}{dz}(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q})(\gamma(y))}{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q_{\kappa,\textnormal{diff}}}(\gamma(y))}\Bigl|\in\mathbb{R}^{*}.

Proof

We have

\frac{d}{dz}{\sigma_{l}z_{l}}=\frac{\sigma_{l}}{\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}z_{j}}.

We obtain

\frac{d}{dz}{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}q_{j}(\sigma_{j}z_{j})^{q_{j}-1}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}z_{i}}\prod_{i\neq j}(\sigma_{i}z_{i})^{q_{i}}.

Consider for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
with
l<\kappa
or
q_{l}\neq 0

p_{l}^{*}:=(q_{0}-1,...,q_{l}-1,q_{l+1},...,q_{r})-q_{\kappa,\textnormal{diff}}.

Then
p_{l}^{*}
is
\kappa
-positive if
l\neq j_{\kappa}(q)
. (This is clear for
l<\kappa
. If
l=\kappa
then
q_{\kappa}\neq 0
and therefore
l=j_{\kappa}(q)
. So suppose that
0\leq k^{\textnormal{ch}}
and
\kappa=k^{\textnormal{ch}}<l
. If
l\neq j_{\kappa}(q)
we see that
j_{\kappa}(q)<l
. This shows that
p_{l}^{*}
is
\kappa
-positive.) If
l=j_{\kappa}(q)
we see that
(p_{l}^{*})_{\kappa}=...=(p_{l}^{*})_{r}=0
. So we get the assertion with Proposition 5.34.
\blacksquare

5.1.3
Persistence, Regularity and Integrability of Logaritmic Scales in the Complex Setting

As in the globally subanalytic setting in
[
17
]
we need definability results of special parameterized integrals of definable holomorphic extensions of prepared restricted log-exp-analytic functions in one variable. For this we have to consider sets of the form

D:=\{(t,z)\in H\mid N(t)<|{z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)}|<M(t)\}

(where
\kappa=r
or
\kappa\in\{k^{\textnormal{ch}},r\}
if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
) where
M,N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are definable functions with special properties: The ”upper limit”
M
belongs to the class of persistent functions and the ”lower limit”
N
to the class of soft functions (see Definition 5.38 below). It turns out that we can easily define the desired parameterized integral outgoing from such a set
D
. (See page 146 in
[
17
]
for the necessary result on integration in the globally subanalytic setting: There we have
r=0
,
\kappa=0
and
\mu_{0}=\Theta_{0}=\theta
. The ”lower limit” there is
\alpha^{*}
, the ”upper limit”
\omega^{*}
,
D
corresponds to
\Gamma
and
\Delta
describes the domain of the parameterized integral.)

But the domain
\Lambda
of a definable holomorphic extension
F:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
of a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable is determined significantly by iterations of logarithmic scales, exponentials and power series. So it requires deep technical arguments to construct such a set
D
with
D\subset\Lambda
. But we can even find corresponding functions
N
and
M
such that
D\subset\Lambda
and
F(t,z)
is complex log-analytically prepared in
z
on
D
, i.e. of the form
F(t,z)=a(t)(\sigma_{0}z_{0}(t,z))^{q_{0}}\cdot...\cdot(\sigma_{r}z_{r}(t,z))^{q_{r}}U(t,z)
where
z_{0}=z-\Theta_{0}(t),z_{1}=\log(\sigma_{0}z_{0})-\Theta_{1}(t)
,…,
\sigma_{j}\in\{-1,1\}
, the
q_{j}
’s are rational exponents and
U(t,z)
is a function of a special form (compare with Section 5.1.4). Additionally the data of this log-analytical preparation (i.e.
a
and
\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r}
) belongs to a special class of functions which we call regular (see Definition 5.43 below). Regular functions are an efficient generalization of nice functions (see Definition 4.5) and are indispensable for the proof that the unary high parametric global complexification of a real analytic restricted log-exp-analytic function can be chosen in a restricted log-exp-analytically way.

All the necessary definitions and results are given in the parametric setting.

For Section 5.1.3 we fix
l,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
l+m=n
. Let
w:=(w_{1},...,w_{l})
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
and
u:=(u_{1},...,u_{m})
over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be definable with
C\subset X
. Suppose that
X_{w}
is open for every
w\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Fix
\kappa=r
or
\kappa\in\{k^{\textnormal{ch}},r\}
if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
.

5.36 Notation

Let
\pi_{l}:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{l},(w,u,x)\mapsto w
, be the projection on the first
l
coordinates. For
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
and a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H_{w}=\{(u,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}\mid(w,u,z)\in H\}
we set
\gamma_{u}:=(\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{m})
for the first
m
real components and
\gamma_{z}:=\gamma_{m+1}
for the last complex component and if
\gamma_{m+1}(y)\in\mathbb{R}
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we also write
\gamma_{x}
instead of
\gamma_{z}
.

5.37 Definition

We say that a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H_{w}
is compatible with
C_{w}
if the curve
\hat{\gamma}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H,y\mapsto(w,\gamma(y))
, is compatible with
C
.

Persistent and Soft Functions

In this paragraph we define and investigate persistent and soft functions and give examples.

5.38 Definition

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function.

(a)

We say that
g
is
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-persistent in
u
with respect to
X
if
g
is definable and the following holds. Let
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
. Then for every definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
compatible with
C_{w}
with

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in X_{w}
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{x}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(w,\gamma_{u}(y)))=0

we have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}g(w,\gamma_{u}(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cup\{\infty\}
.

(b)

We say that
g
is
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-soft in
u
with respect to
X
if
g
is definable and the following holds. Let
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
. Then for every definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
compatible with
C_{w}
with

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in X_{w}
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{x}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(w,\gamma_{u}(y)))=0

we have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}g(w,\gamma_{u}(y))=0
.

5.39 Remark

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function. Let
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be definable with
X\subset Y
such that
Y_{w}
is open for every
w\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
. If
g
is
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-persistent (
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-soft) in
u
with respect to
Y
then
g
is
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-persistent (
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-soft) in
u
with respect to
X
.

Note that a function which is not
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-persistent in
u
with respect to
X
is not automatically
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-soft in
u
with respect to
X
in general (compare with Example 5.41(1)).

Since
C
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
X
are fixed we often say ”
\kappa
-persistent” respectively ”
\kappa
-soft” instead of ”
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-persistent in
u
with respect to
X
” respectively ”
(C,\mathcal{Y},\kappa)
-soft in
u
with respect to
X
” in Section 5.1.3. In the following we give some elementary facts and examples.

5.40 Remark

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a definable function. If for every
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
and every definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
compatible with
C_{w}
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in X_{w}
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\textnormal{dist}(\mu_{\kappa}(w,\gamma_{u}(y)),(C_{w})_{\gamma_{u}(y)})=0

we have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}g(w,\gamma_{u}(y))>0
(
\lim_{y\searrow 0}g(w,\gamma_{u}(y))=0
) then
g
is
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft).

5.41 Example

Let
w=0
and
u=1
. Let
X=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
l=0
. Let
C
and
\mathcal{Y}=(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})
be as in Example 5.10 (i.e.
r=2
and
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=1
). Consider the definable functions
g_{1}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},u\mapsto u,g_{2}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},u\mapsto 1-u,
and

g_{3}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},u\mapsto(u-1/2)^{2}.
The following holds.

(1)

Let
X=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
g_{1}
is
2
-persistent,
g_{2}
is
2
-soft and
g_{3}
is neither
1
-persistent nor
1
-soft.

(2)

Let
X=C
. Then
g_{1},g_{2}
and
g_{3}
are
\kappa
-persistent and
\kappa
-soft for
\kappa\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof

We have

C=\{(u,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{},\textnormal{}\tfrac{1}{1+u}+e^{-u-1/u}<x<\tfrac{1}{1+u}+e^{-1/u}\}.

Note that
C
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we have
L_{C}(u)=e^{-1/u}(1-e^{-u})
. Note that
L_{C}(u)>0
for
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
, that
\lim_{u\searrow 0}L_{C}(u)=0
and that
\lim_{u\nearrow 1}L_{C}(u)>0
.
For
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we have
\mu_{1}(u)=\frac{1}{1+u}+e^{-1/u}
and
\mu_{2}(u)=\frac{1}{1+u}+e^{-1-1/u}
.

(1): We see that

\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\textnormal{dist}(\mu_{2}(u),C_{u})=\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\bigl(e^{-u-1/u}-e^{-1-1/u}\bigl)=0

and if
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
we see that
\textnormal{dist}(\mu_{2}(u),C_{u})>0
. So with Remark 5.40 we see that
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
fulfill the desired properties since
\lim_{u\nearrow 1}g_{1}(u)>0
and
\lim_{u\nearrow 1}g_{2}(u)=0
. One sees that if a function on
\pi(C)
is
1
-persistent it does not have any zero and that the only
1
-soft function on
\pi(C)
is the zero function. (Since
\textnormal{dist}(\mu_{1}(u),C_{u})=0
for
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
: For every
u\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
there is a definable function
f_{u}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{u}
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f_{u}(y)-\mu_{1}(u)=0
. So
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C,y\mapsto(u,f_{u}(y)),
is a definable curve compatible with
C
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{x}(y)-\mu_{1}(u)=0
.) So
g_{3}
is neither
1
-persistent nor
1
-soft.

(2): Note that there is no definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{u}(y)\in C
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\gamma_{x}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{u}(y)))=0.

So the assertion follows.

\blacksquare

5.42 Remark

(1)

The set of all
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft) functions on
\pi(C)
is closed under pointwise addition and multiplication.

(2)

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft) and let
q\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\sqrt[q]{g(t)}
, is
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft).

(3)

Let
f_{1},f_{2}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft). Then

\pi(C)\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\min\{f_{1}(t),f_{2}(t)\},

and

\pi(C)\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\max\{f_{1}(t),f_{2}(t)\},

are
\kappa
-persistent (
\kappa
-soft).

5.43 Example

Let
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
l\in\{1,...,r\}
and
j\in\{0,...,l-1\}
. Then

\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},t\mapsto e^{\mu_{j,l}(t)},

and

\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},t\mapsto|{\mu_{l}(t)-\mu_{j}(t)}|,

are
\kappa
-persistent.

Proof

Let
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
. Then for every definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
compatible with
C_{w}
we have
\lim_{y\searrow 0}e^{\mu_{j,l}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))}>0
by Corollary 5.29(1) and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mu_{l}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))-\mu_{j}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))}|>0

if
j\neq l
by Corollary 5.29(2).
\blacksquare

Since the logarithms of the exponentials which occur in a
(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically prepared function
f:C\to\mathbb{R},(w,u,x)\mapsto f(w,u,x),
are locally bounded in
(u,x)
with respect to
X
(see Definition 4.10 and Proposition 4.12 above) we can make some statements about the
\kappa
-persistence and
\kappa
-softness of the data of such a preparation.

5.44 Proposition

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R},(w,u,x)\mapsto f(w,u,x),
be
(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
with preparing tuple

(r,\mathcal{Y},a,\mathrm{exp}(c),q,s,v,b,\mathrm{exp}(d),P)

with
b=(b_{1},...,b_{s})
,
\mathrm{exp}(d)=(\mathrm{exp}(d_{1}),...,\mathrm{exp}(d_{s}))
and
P=(p_{1},...,p_{s})^{t}
. Then the following holds.

(1)

Let
j\in\{1,...,s\}
. The function
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},t\mapsto|{1/b_{j}(t)}|,
is
\kappa
-persistent. Additionally if
p_{j}
is
\kappa
-negative then
|{b_{j}}|
is
\kappa
-soft.

(2)

Suppose that
a\neq 0
and that
f
is locally bounded in
(u,x)
with reference set
X
. Then the function
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},t\mapsto|{1/a(t)}|,
is
\kappa
-persistent. Additionally if
q
is
\kappa
-negative then
|{a}|
is
\kappa
-soft.

Proof

For
(t,x)\in C
let

V(t,x):=v(b_{1}(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes p_{1}}e^{d_{1}(t,x)},...,b_{s}(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes p_{s}}e^{d_{s}(t,x)}).

For
(t,x)\in C
we have

f(t,x)=a(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes q}e^{c(t,x)}V(t,x).

Let
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
and
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
be a definable curve compatible with
C_{w}
such that
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{u}(y)\in X_{w}
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\gamma_{x}(y)-\mu_{\kappa}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))}|=0.

We find
\delta>0
and
B>0
such that
U:=Q^{m}(u_{0},\delta)\times\textnormal{}]x_{0}-\delta,x_{0}+\delta[\textnormal{}\subset X_{w}
,
|{c(w,u,x)}|<B
and
|{d_{j}(w,u,x)}|<B
for
j\in\{1,...,s\}
and every
(u,x)\in U
. By passing to a suitable subcurve of
\gamma
if necessary we may assume that
\gamma(y)\in Q^{m}(u_{0},\delta)\times\textnormal{}]x_{0}-\delta/2,x_{0}+\delta/2[
for every
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
. Similarly as in the proof of Remark 5.27(1) we find a continuous definable function
\tilde{\gamma}_{x}\textnormal{}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}:=(\gamma_{u},\tilde{\gamma}_{x})
is a definable curve in
C_{w}\cap U
compatible with
C_{w}
and for every
l\in\{0,...,r\}
we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(y)-\mu_{l}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))}|>0.

(1): Let
j\in\{1,...,s\}
. Note that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mathcal{Y}(w,\tilde{\gamma}(y))}|^{\otimes p_{j}}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

by Corollary 5.31(1). Since

b_{j}(t)|{\mathcal{Y}(t,x)}|^{\otimes p_{j}}e^{d_{j}(t,x)}\in[-1,1]

for every
(t,x)\in C
we obtain
\lim_{y\searrow 0}b_{j}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
. This gives the first part.

Suppose that
p_{j}
is
\kappa
-negative. Then by Proposition 5.34(2) and Remark 5.15(1) we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mathcal{Y}(w,\gamma(y))}|^{\otimes p_{j}}=\infty

and therefore

\lim_{y\searrow 0}b_{j}(w,\gamma_{u}(y))=0.

(2): Note that there is
\rho>1
such that
V(t,x)\in\textnormal{}]1/\rho,\rho[
for every
(t,x)\in C
and that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mathcal{Y}(w,\tilde{\gamma}(y))}|^{\otimes q}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

by Corollary 5.31(1). Since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f(w,\tilde{\gamma}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
we obtain

\lim_{y\searrow 0}a(w,\gamma_{u}(y))\in\mathbb{R}.

This gives the first part.

Suppose that
q
is
\kappa
-negative. Then by Proposition 5.34(2) and Remark 5.15(1) we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mathcal{Y}(w,\gamma(y))}|^{\otimes q}=\infty

and therefore
\lim_{y\searrow 0}a(w,\gamma_{u}(y))=0
since
\lim_{y\searrow 0}f(w,\gamma_{u}(y))\in\mathbb{R}
.

\blacksquare

Note that in the globally subanalytic setting in
[
17
]
on page 149
\alpha^{*}
defines a
\kappa
-soft function and
\omega^{*}
a
\kappa
-persistent function which can be verified easily with Proposition 5.44(1). (There we have
r=0
and therefore
\kappa=0
.)

5.45 Definition

We call
C

near with respect to
\mu_{\kappa}
if there is a definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C
compatible with
C
such that

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\mu_{\kappa}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\gamma_{x}(y))=0
. Otherwise we call
C

far with respect to
\mu_{\kappa}
.

Figure 5.5:
The functions
\mu_{j}
for a
1
-logarithmic scale
\mathcal{Y}
with
\textnormal{sign}(\mathcal{Y})=(1,-1)
where
C
is far with respect to
\mu_{0}
and near with respect to
\mu_{1}
. (Here we have
k^{\textnormal{ch}}=0
and
r=1
.)

5.46 Remark

If
C
is far with respect to
\mu_{\kappa}
then every definable function
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is
\kappa
-persistent and
\kappa
-soft.

So if it is desired to find a
\kappa
-persistent function
M
and a
\kappa
-soft function
N
such that
G|_{D}
fulfills specific properties (where
G:H\to\mathbb{C}
is definable and

D:=\{(t,z)\in H\mid N(t)<|{z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)}|<M(t)\})

it is enough to look at cells which are near with respect to
\mu_{\kappa}
. In the case that
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
and
\kappa=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
the boundedness condition for
\Theta
gives the following result which simplifies technical proofs below enormously.

5.47 Proposition

Let
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
and let
C
be near with respect to
\mu_{k}
. Then
\Theta_{l}=0
for every
l\in\{k+1,...,r\}
.

Proof

Let
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C
be a definable curve compatible with
C
and let

\lim_{y\searrow 0}(\mu_{k}(\gamma_{t}(y))-\gamma_{x}(y))=0.

Then
\lim_{y\searrow 0}y_{k}(\gamma(y))=0
by
(II_{k})
in Proposition 5.28. We do an induction on
l\in\{k+1,...,r\}
.

l=k+1
: Assume
\Theta_{k+1}\neq 0
. Then there is
\epsilon_{k+1}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that

|{\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(t,x))-\Theta_{k+1}(t)}|<\epsilon_{k+1}|{\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(t,x))}|

for all
(t,x)\in C
. We obtain

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\Bigl|1-\frac{\Theta_{k+1}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(\gamma(y)))}\Bigl|\leq\epsilon_{k+1}

and therefore
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\Theta_{k+1}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|=\infty
, a contradiction to
(I_{k+1})
in Proposition 5.28.

l\to l+1
: Note that
\sigma_{k+1}=-1
and
\sigma_{k+2}=...=\sigma_{l}=1
. So we obtain

\log(\sigma_{l}y_{l})=\log_{l-k}(-\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}))

on
C
by the inductive hypothesis. Assume
\Theta_{l+1}\neq 0
. Then there is
\epsilon_{l+1}\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
such that

|{\log_{l-k}(-\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(t,x)))-\Theta_{l+1}(t)}|<\epsilon_{l+1}|{\log_{l-k}(-\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(t,x)))}|

for all
(t,x)\in C
. We obtain

\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\Bigl|1-\frac{\Theta_{l+1}(\gamma_{t}(y))}{\log_{l-k}(-\log(\sigma_{k}y_{k}(\gamma(y))))}\Bigl|\leq\epsilon_{l+1}

and therefore
\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\Theta_{l+1}(\gamma_{t}(y))}|=\infty
, a contradiction to
(I_{l+1})
in Proposition 5.28.

\blacksquare

Regular Functions

In this paragraph we define regular functions on
\pi(C)
as compositions of log-analytic functions and exponentials of functions which fulfill a special boundedness condition depending on
X
. (For example the center
\Theta
of a logarithmic scale on
C
fulfills this condition.)

As above we give the definitions in the parametric setting.

5.48 Definition

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
be definable. We call
g

C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
if the following holds. Let
w\in\pi_{l}(C)
. Then for every definable curve
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to C_{w}
compatible with
C_{w}
with
\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma(y)\in X_{w}
we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}g(w,\gamma_{u}(y))\in\mathbb{R}.

So a
C
-consistent function
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
in
u
with respect to
X
fulfills the following: If for
(w_{0},u_{0})\in\pi(X)
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
u_{0}
in
\pi(X)_{w_{0}}
and
\epsilon,c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that
L_{C}(w_{0},u)>\epsilon
,
\inf(C_{(w_{0},u)})<c_{1}
and
\sup(C_{(w_{0},u)})>c_{2}
for every
u\in\pi(C)_{w_{0}}\cap U
then
g_{w_{0}}
is bounded at
u_{0}
, i.e. there is
V\subset U
open with
u_{0}\in V
such that
g_{w_{0}}|_{\pi(C)_{w_{0}}\cap V}
is bounded. If
C=X
then
g
is locally bounded in
u
.

Figure 5.6:
Let
l=0
. A
C
-consistent function
f:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
in
u
with respect to
X
is bounded at
u_{0}
, but not necessarily bounded at
u_{1}
respectively
u_{2}
.

5.49 Example

Let
l=0
,
m=1
,
C_{1}:=\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}]0,1[
and

C_{2}:=\{(u,x)\in\textnormal{}]0,1[\times\mathbb{R}\mid 0<x<u\}.

Then
]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto 1/u,
is
C_{1}
-consistent in
u
with respect to
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}
, but not
C_{1}
-consistent in
u
with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that this function is
C_{2}
-consistent with respect to any open
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
C_{2}\subset X
.

In the following we give some elementary properties of
C
-consistent functions in
u
with respect to
X
.

5.50 Remark

Let
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
be definable with
X\subset Y
such that
Y_{w}
is open for every
w\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
. A function
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
which is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
Y
is also
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
.

5.51 Example

(1)

The functions
\Theta_{0},...,\Theta_{r}
are
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

Let
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. The function
\pi(C)\mapsto\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\mu_{j,l}(t),
is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
for
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

(3)

The logarithm of a
C
-heir is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof

(1): Follows from
(I_{j})
in Proposition 5.28 for
j\in\{0,...,r\}
.

(2): Follows from Corollary 5.29(1).

(3): Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a
C
-heir. Note that there is
l\in\mathbb{N}
, an
l
-logarithmic scale
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
with center
(\hat{\Theta}_{0},...,\hat{\Theta}_{l})
such that
g=\mathrm{exp}(\hat{\Theta}_{j})
for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
. So this property follows from
(I_{j})
in Proposition 5.28 applied to
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
.

\blacksquare

5.52 Remark

(1)

The set of
C
-consistent functions in
u
with respect to
X
is an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with respect to pointwise addition and multiplication.

(2)

The set of non-negative
C
-consistent functions in
u
with respect to
X
is a divisible monoid with respect to pointwise multiplication.

(3)

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Let
f:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
be
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
. Then
\mathrm{exp}(q\cdot f)
is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
.

(4)

Let
g:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
. Then
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0},t\mapsto 1/g(t)
, is
\kappa
-persistent (where
\kappa=r
or
\kappa\in\{k^{\textnormal{ch}},r\}
if
k^{\textnormal{ch}}\geq 0
).

A very important observation is that most of the data of an
(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically prepared function is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
(since the logarithms of the exponentials in such a preparation are locally bounded in
(u,x)
with respect to
X
(compare with Definition 4.10 and Proposition 4.12 above)).

5.53 Remark

Let
e\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
f:C\to\mathbb{R},(w,u,x)\mapsto f(w,u,x),
be
(m+1,X)
-restricted log-exp-analytically
(e,r)
-prepared in
x
with preparing tuple

(r,\mathcal{Y},a,\mathrm{exp}(c),q,s,v,b,\mathrm{exp}(d),P)

with
b=(b_{1},...,b_{s})
,
\mathrm{exp}(d)=(\mathrm{exp}(d_{1}),...,\mathrm{exp}(d_{s}))
and
P=(p_{1},...,p_{s})^{t}
. Then the following holds.

(1)

Let
j\in\{1,...,s\}
. The function
b_{j}
is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
.

(2)

Assume that
f
is locally bounded in
(u,x)
with reference set
X
. The function
a
is
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
.

Proof

Compare with the proof of Proposition 5.44.
\blacksquare

5.54 Definition

A function
f:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
is called
C
-regular in
u
with respect to
X
if there is a set
E
of positive definable functions on
\pi(C)
such that the following holds. The set
\log(E)
consists only of
C
-consistent functions in
u
with respect to
X
and
f
can be constructed from
E
.

Throughout Section 5.1.3 we say ”consistent” respectively ”regular” instead of ”
C
-consistent in
u
with respect to
X
” respectively ”
C
-regular in
u
with respect to
X
” since
C
and
X
are fixed.

With Remark 3.4 we obtain the following.

5.55 Remark

(1)

Let
f:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
be regular and consistent. Then
\mathrm{exp}(f)
is regular and consistent.

(2)

Let
f_{1},...,f_{m}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
be regular. Let
G:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be log-analytic. Then
G(f_{1},...,f_{m})
is regular.

Since the logarithmic scale which occurs in a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function has a
C
-nice center, we assume for the rest of Section 5.1 that
\Theta
is
C
-nice and therefore also regular.

5.56 Example

(1)

Let
l\in\{0,...,r\}
. The function
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\mu_{j,l}(t),
is
C
-regular and
C
-consistent for
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

(2)

The function
\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa-1}e^{q_{j}\mu_{j,\kappa}(t)},
is
C
-regular and
C
-consistent for
q_{0},...,q_{\kappa-1}\in\mathbb{Q}
.

(3)

Suppose that
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is open with
0\in X
. Let
C:=\textnormal{}]0,1[^{2}
and let
h:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R},u\mapsto e^{-1/u}
. Then
h
is not
C
-regular.

Proof

(1): By Remark 5.55(1), Remark 5.52(1) and an easy induction on
j\in\{0,...,l\}
we see that
\mu_{j,l}
is
C
-regular and
C
-consistent.

(2): With (1) and Remark 5.55(1) we obtain that
e^{\mu_{j,l}}
is
C
-regular. With Remark 5.55(2) we obtain that
\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa-1}e^{q_{j}\mu_{j,\kappa}(t)}
is
C
-regular. (By considering the gobally subanalytic function
\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R},(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{l})\mapsto\alpha_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdot...\cdot\alpha_{l}^{q_{l}}
.)
Note that
\mathrm{exp}(\mu_{j,l})
is
C
-consistent. Therefore
\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa-1}e^{q_{j}\mu_{j,\kappa}(t)}
is
C
-consistent (by Remark 5.52(1)/(3)).

(3): Note that
\pi(C)=\textnormal{}]0,1[
. Suppose the contrary. Let
E
be a set of
C
-regular functions with respect to
X
such that
h
can be constructed from
E
. Note that for every
g\in\log(E)
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
0
in
\pi(X)
such that
g|_{U\cap]0,1[}
is bounded. Therefore
h\sim\beta
for a log-analytic
\beta:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{R}
at zero. But we have
h\sim e^{-1/u}
at zero, a contradiction.
\blacksquare

Integrability of Logarithmic Scales

We close this subsection by establishing some definability results of parameterized integrals of logarithmic scales in the complex setting. With the preparation result in Section 5.1.4 one gets then easily the desired result on integration for definable holomorphic extensions of prepared restricted log-exp-analytic functions in one variable. In Section 5.2 we use such results to construct the unary high parametric global complexification.

5.57 Notation

Let
s
and
\tau
range over
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\pi^{+}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},(t,s,z)\mapsto(t,s),
be the projection on the first
n+1
real coordinates.

For a definable function
g:Y\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto g(t,s,\tau),
where
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
is definable we say that
g
is bounded in
\tau
if
g_{(t,s)}
is bounded on
Y_{(t,s)}=\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}\mid(t,s,\tau)\in Y\}
for every
(t,s)\in\pi^{+}(Y)
.

5.58 Convention

For
(t,s)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we parameterize the circle
\partial B(\mu_{\kappa}(t),s)\subset\mathbb{C}
with the counterclockwise oriented curve

\rho:[-\pi,\pi]\to\mathbb{R},\tau\mapsto\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}.

5.59 Definition

For definable functions
N,M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a definable function
G:H\to\mathbb{C}
we set

\mathcal{B}(G,M):=\{(t,z)\in H\mid|{G(t,z)}|<M(t)\}

and

\mathcal{A}(G,N,M):=\{(t,z)\in H\mid N(t)<|{G(t,z)}|<M(t)\}.

5.60 Remark

Let
N,M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be definable. For a definable
G:H\to\mathbb{C}
the sets
\mathcal{B}(G,M)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}
and
\mathcal{A}(G,N,M)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}
are definable. We have

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},M)=\{(t,z)\in H\mid 0<|{z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)}|<M(t)\}

and

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)=\{(t,z)\in H\mid N(t)<|{z-\mu_{\kappa}(t)}|<M(t)\}.

5.61 Definition

Let
D\subset\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
be definable. We set

D(\mu_{\kappa}):=\bigl\{(t,s,\tau)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times[-\pi,\pi]\mid\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}\in D_{t}\bigl\}.

For the rest of Section 5.1.3 let
k:=k^{\textnormal{ch}}
.

5.62 Remark

Let
M,N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be definable functions. Suppose that
M<|{\mu_{r}-\mu_{k}}|
if
k\geq 0
. Let
D:=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)
. Then

D(\mu_{\kappa})=\{(t,s)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mid N(t)<s<M(t)\}\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[.

Proof

Let

B:=\{(t,s)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mid N(t)<s<M(t)\}\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[.

Suppose
\kappa=r
. Let
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=1
. The case ”
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j}=-1
” is treated completely similar. For
t\in\pi(C)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\tau\in\textnormal{}[-\pi,\pi]
we set
\delta(t,s,\tau):=\mu_{r}(t)+se^{i\tau}
. Note that we have

\delta(t,s,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{r}(t)}
if
k<0
respectively
\delta(t,s,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{r}(t)}\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}_{\mu_{k}(t)}
if
k\geq 0
for
(t,s,\tau)\in B
. Remark 5.13 gives
B\subset D(\mu_{\kappa})
. Let
(t,s,\tau)\in D(\mu_{\kappa})
. Then
t\in\pi(C)
,
N(t)<s<M(t)
and since
\delta(t,s,\tau)\in D_{t}
we have
\tau\in\textnormal{]}-\pi,\pi[
. (If
\tau\in\{-\pi,\pi\}
we obtain
\delta(t,s,\tau)=\mu_{r}(t)-s<\mu_{r}(t)
, i.e.
\delta(t,s,\tau)\notin\mathbb{C}^{-}_{\mu_{r}}
and therefore
\delta(t,s,\tau)\notin D_{t}
by Remark 5.13.) We obtain
(t,s,\tau)\in B
. The case ”
\kappa=k
” is treatened completely similar.
\blacksquare

5.63 Proposition

Let
M,N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be definable functions. Suppose that
M<|{\mu_{r}-\mu_{k}}|
if
k\geq 0
. Let
D:=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)
. Let
F:D\to\mathbb{C}
be definable such that

F^{*}:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto F(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t)+\bigl(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j}\bigl)se^{i\tau}),

is bounded in
\tau
. Suppose that
F^{*}
is globally subanalytic in
\tau
with support function
g:\pi^{+}(D(\mu_{\kappa}))\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
for an
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let

\Delta:=\{(t,s,z)\in\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{C}\mid N(t)<s<M(t),z\in B(\mu_{\kappa}(t),s)\}.

Then the function

\Psi:\Delta\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,z)\mapsto\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial B(\mu_{\kappa}(t),s)}\frac{F(t,\xi)}{\xi-z}d\xi,

is well-defined and constructible in
z
with support function

h:\pi^{+}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},(t,s)\mapsto(g(t,s),\mu_{\kappa}(t),s).

Proof

Let
G:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be globally subanalytic such that for
(t,s,\tau)\in D(\mu_{\kappa})

F^{*}(t,s,\tau)=G(g(t,s),\tau).

For
(t,s,z)\in\Delta
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(\mu_{\kappa}(t),s)}\frac{F(t,\xi)}{\xi-z}d\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{F^{*}(t,s,\tau)}{\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}-z}i(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}d\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{G(g(t,s),\tau)}{\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}-z}i(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}d\tau.

Note that

\Delta\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,z,\tau)\mapsto\frac{(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}}{\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}-z},

is bounded in
\tau
. So
\Psi
is well-defined.

Let
v:=(v_{1},...,v_{l})
range over
\mathbb{R}^{l}
and
y
over
\mathbb{R}
. Set

Q:=\{(v,x,y,z,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{C}\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[\textnormal{}\mid|{x-z}|<y\}

and

\Omega:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C},(v,x,y,z,\tau)\mapsto

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{G(v,\tau)}{x+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})ye^{i\tau}-z}i(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})ye^{i\tau},&(v,x,y,z,\tau)\in Q,\\
0,&\textnormal{else.}\end{array}\right.

Then
\Omega
is globally subanalytic since we have that

]-\pi,\pi[\textnormal{}\to\mathbb{C},\tau\mapsto e^{i\tau}=\cos(\tau)+i\sin(\tau),

is globally subanalytic. Then for
(t,s,z,\tau)\in\Delta\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[
we have
|{\mu_{\kappa}(t)-z}|<s
and therefore
(h(t,s),z,\tau)\in Q
. So we obtain

\frac{G(g(t,s),\tau)}{\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}-z}i(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}=\Omega(h(t,s),z,\tau)

for every
(t,s,z,\tau)\in\Delta\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[
. By Remark 1.26 there is a constructible
G:\mathbb{R}^{l}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\Psi(t,s,z)=G(h(t,s),z)
for
(t,s,z)\in\Delta
. So we see that
\Psi
is constructible in
z
with support function
h
.
\blacksquare

Note the following: For
(t,s)\in\pi^{+}(\Delta)
the parameterized integral
\Psi
coincides with
F_{t}
by Cauchy’s integral formula if
F_{t}
can be extended to a holomorphic function on
B(\mu_{\kappa}(t),\tilde{s})
where
s<\tilde{s}<M(t)
.

In our treatment below the initial function
F
is a holomorphic extension of a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable. (In the globally subanalytic setting
F
is the holomorphic extension of a prepared globally subanalytic function in one variable (see Proposition 2.8 in
[
17
]
). There we have
\kappa=0
and
\mu_{\kappa}=\theta
.) Outgoing from Proposition 5.63 we obtain the following

Strategy:

For a definable holomorphic extension
F:\Gamma\to\mathbb{C}
of a prepared restricted log-exp-analytic function in one variable which we will construct in Section 5.1.4 we have to find a
\kappa
-persistent
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a
\kappa
-soft
N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
F^{*}|_{\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)}
is bounded and globally subanalytic in
\tau
. In the construction of the unary high parametric global complexification in Section 5.2 below the parameter
s
will be ”replaced” by a function
\alpha:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
which arises in a natural way from
N
and
M
. For further considerations it is necessary that the function
\Psi(t,\alpha(t),z)
which we obtain after this replacement is constructible in
z
with regular support function (according to the preparation result in Section 5.1.4). This is the case if
M
and
N
are regular and
F^{*}
is free-regular in
s
.

5.64 Definition

Let
D\subset\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
be definable. We call a function
f:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}

free-regular in
s
if there is
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
f
is globally subanalytic in
\tau
with support function
g:=(g_{1},...,g_{l}):\pi^{+}(D(\mu_{\kappa}))\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
and the following holds for
g
.
There is
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, a regular function
\beta:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and for every
j\in\{1,...,l\}
a log-analytic
h_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{p+1}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
g_{j}(t,s)=h_{j}(\beta(t),s)
for every
(t,s)\in\pi^{+}(D(\mu_{\kappa}))
.

5.65 Remark

Let
D\subset\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
be definable.

(1)

Let
f:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic. Then
f
is free-regular in
s
.

(2)

Let
f:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic in
\tau
with log-analytic support function. Then
f
is free-regular in
s
.

5.66 Remark

Let
D\subset\pi(C)\times\mathbb{C}
be definable. Let
f:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}
be free-regular in
s
. The following holds.

(1)

f
is globally subanalytic in
\tau
with support function
g:\pi^{+}(D(\mu_{\kappa}))\to\mathbb{R}^{l}
which can be constructed from a set
E
of positive definable functions such that every
h\in\log(E)
is consistent.

(2)

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and let
G:\mathbb{C}^{q}\to\mathbb{C}
be globally subanalytic. Let
J_{1},...,J_{q}:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}
be functions which are free-regular in
s
. Then
H:=G(J_{1},...,J_{q}):D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C}
is free-regular in
s
.

Proof

(1): Let
g:=(g_{1},...,g_{l})
. Definition 5.64, Definition 5.54 and Remark 5.55(2) show immediately that
g_{j}
can be constructed from a set
E
of positive definable functions such that every
h\in\log(E)
is consistent for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
.

(2): Follows directly with Remark 1.25(2).
\blacksquare

In the rest of the section we focus on the case that our initial function
F
in Proposition 5.63 above is
(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. The first step here is to find a regular
\kappa
-persistent
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a regular
\kappa
-soft
N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

z_{l}^{*}:\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto z_{j}(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is free-regular in
s
for
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. (We will see below that we can choose
N=0
.) This can be done with the following technical result which is also needed to establish the desired preparation result in Section 5.1.4.

5.67 Proposition

Let
U:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be regular and
\kappa
-persistent. Then the following holds.

(1)

Let
0<\delta\leq 1/2
. There is a regular
\kappa
-persistent
\zeta:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
K:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},\zeta)\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa},U)
for
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
and

\Bigl|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-j,\kappa}(t)}}\Bigl|<\delta

for every
(t,z)\in K
and every
j\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
. If
U>0
then
\zeta>0
.

(2)

Let
W:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be regular and
\kappa
-soft. Then there is a regular
\kappa
-persistent
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a regular
\kappa
-soft
N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)\subset\mathcal{A}(z_{\kappa},W,U)
.

Proof

We start with the following claim.

Claim

Let
0<\delta\leq 1/2
. There are globally subanalytic functions
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\textnormal{}[0,\delta]
such that the following holds.

(i)

\lambda_{j}
is monotone increasing for
j\in\{1,2\}
.

(ii)

For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have
|{\log(1+z)}|<r
if
|{z}|<\lambda_{1}(r)
.

(iii)

For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have
r<|{\log(1+z)}|
if
\lambda_{2}(r)<|{z}|<\delta
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

(iv)

We have
\lambda_{2}(0)=0
,
\lim_{r\searrow 0}\lambda_{2}(r)=0
and
\lim_{r\nearrow r_{0}}\lambda_{j}(r)>0
for
r_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof of the claim

Define

\lambda_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\textnormal{}[0,\delta],r\mapsto

\sup\{c\in\textnormal{}[0,\delta[\textnormal{}\mid\textnormal{for every $z\in\mathbb{C}$ with }|{z}|<c\textnormal{ it holds }|{\log^{*}(1+z)}|<r\}.

Note that
\lambda_{1}
is well-defined, monotone increasing and we have
\lambda_{1}(0)=0
. For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
set

s(r):=\inf\{c\in\textnormal{}[0,\delta[\textnormal{}\mid\textnormal{for every $z\in\mathbb{C}$ with }c<|{z}|<\delta\textnormal{ it holds }r<|{\log^{*}(1+z)}|\}.

Note that
s(r_{1})\leq s(r_{2})
for
0\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}
. Set

\lambda_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to[0,\delta],r\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s(r),&s(r)\neq\infty,\\
\delta,&\textnormal{else.}\end{array}\right.

Note that
\lambda_{j}
is globally subanalytic since
\log^{*}
is globally subanalytic and fulfills the desired properties for
j\in\{1,2\}
.

\blacksquare_{\textnormal{Claim}}

(1): Let
\lambda_{1}
be as in the claim. Let
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
. Let
\zeta_{l,l}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto U(t)
. Define by descending induction on
j\in\{1,...,l\}

\zeta_{j-1,l}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\lambda_{1}(\zeta_{j,l}(t))e^{\mu_{\kappa-j,\kappa}(t)}.

By an easy descending induction on
j\in\{0,...,l\}
one sees with the claim that
\zeta_{j,l}
is a well-defined regular
\kappa
-persistent function (compare with Remark 5.42(1) and Example 5.43). If
|{\mathcal{P}_{j-1,\kappa}(t,z)}|<\zeta_{j-1,l}(t)
then

\Bigl|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-j,\kappa}(t)}}\Bigl|<\lambda_{1}(\zeta_{j,l}(t))\leq\delta

and therefore

|{\mathcal{P}_{j,\kappa}(t,z)}|=|{\log\bigl(1+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-j,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)|}<\zeta_{j,l}(t)

for
j\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
and
(t,z)\in H
by definition of
\lambda_{1}
. So we take

\zeta:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\min\{\zeta_{0,0}(t),...,\zeta_{0,\kappa}(t)\}.

(2): Let
0<\delta\leq 1/2
. Let
\lambda_{2}
be as in the claim. By (1) there is a regular
\kappa
-persistent
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

K:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},M)\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{\kappa},U)=\mathcal{B}(z_{\kappa},U)
and we have

\Bigl|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\Bigl|<\delta

for every
(t,z)\in K
and every
l\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
. Let
N_{\kappa}:=W
. Define by descending induction on
l\in\{1,...,\kappa\}

N_{l-1}:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\lambda_{2}(N_{l}(t))e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}.

By an easy descending induction on
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
one sees that
N_{l}
is a well-defined regular
\kappa
-soft function. This gives the following: If
N_{l-1}(t)<|{\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}|
then

\lambda_{2}(N_{l}(t))<\Bigl|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\Bigl|<\delta

and therefore

N_{l}(t)<|{\log\bigl(1+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl)|}=|{\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,z)|}

for
l\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
and
(t,z)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},M)
by definition of
\lambda_{2}
. So set
N:=N_{0}
. We obtain
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},N,M)\subset\mathcal{A}(z_{\kappa},W,U)
. This finishes the proof of Proposition 5.67.
\blacksquare

5.68 Corollary

There is a regular
\kappa
-persistent function
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
M<|{\mu_{r}-\mu_{k}}|
if
k\geq 0
such that for every
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
the following holds where
D:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},M)
.

(1)

The function

\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}^{*}:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is free-regular in
s
.

(2)

The function

z_{l}^{*}:D(\mu_{\kappa})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto z_{l}(t,\mu_{\kappa}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is free-regular in
s
.

Proof

By Proposition 5.67(1) there is a regular
\kappa
-persistent
T:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\Bigl|\frac{\mathcal{P}_{j-1,\kappa}(t,z)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-j,\kappa}(t)}}\Bigl|<1/2

for
(t,z)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},T)
for every
j\in\{1,...,\kappa\}
. Let
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\min\{|{\mu_{r}(t)-\mu_{k}(t)}|,T(t)\}
and
D:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa},M)
.
Note that a regular function
h:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}
considered as a function on
D(\mu_{\kappa})
is free-regular in
s
.

(1): We do an induction on
l
. Note that
\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}^{*}
is globally subanalytic, because

\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau)=(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}=(\prod_{j=0}^{\kappa}\sigma_{j})s(\cos(\tau)+i\sin(\tau))

for
(t,s,\tau)\in D(\mu_{\kappa})
. So
\mathcal{P}_{0,\kappa}^{*}
is free-regular in
s
by Remark 5.65(1).

l-1\to l:
For
(t,s,\tau)\in D(\mu_{\kappa})
we obtain

\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau)=\log^{*}\bigl(1+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{l-1,\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau)}{e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}(t)}}\bigl).

Because
\log^{*}
is globally subanalytic we see with Remark 5.66(2) that
\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}^{*}
is free-regular in
s
since
e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}}
is regular (and therefore
e^{\mu_{\kappa-l,\kappa}}
is also free-regular in
s
considered as a function on
D(\mu_{\kappa})
since coordinate projections are globally subanalytic).

(2): For
(t,s,\tau)\in D(\mu_{\kappa})
we obtain with Proposition 5.18

z_{l}^{*}(t,s,\tau)=\mathcal{P}_{l,\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau)+\sigma_{l}e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}(t)}

for
l\in\{0,...,\kappa-1\}
and with Corollary 5.19

z_{\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau)=\mathcal{P}_{\kappa,\kappa}^{*}(t,s,\tau).

So we obtain that
z_{l}^{*}
is free-regular in
s
for every
l\in\{0,...,\kappa\}
with (1) if
l=\kappa
respectively (1) and Remark 5.66(2) if
l\in\{0,...,\kappa-1\}
since
e^{\mu_{\kappa-l-1,\kappa}}
is regular.

\blacksquare

Now we obtain the desired result on integration for a logarithmic scale in the complex setting if
\kappa=r
.

5.69 Corollary

Let
\kappa=r
. Let
q:=(q_{0},...,q_{r})\in\mathbb{Q}^{r+1}
. There is a regular
r
-persistent function
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
M<|{\mu_{r}-\mu_{k}}|
if
k\geq 0
such that for
D:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,r},M)
the function

\mathcal{Z}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}:D(\mu_{r})\mapsto\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(t,\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is bounded in
\tau
and free-regular in
s
.

Proof

By Corollary 5.68(2) we find a regular
r
-persistent
T:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

z_{l}^{*}:\hat{D}(\mu_{r})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto z_{l}(t,\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is free-regular in
s
for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
where
\hat{D}:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,r},T)
. Then
\sigma_{l}z_{l}^{*}(t,s,\tau)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
for every
(t,s,\tau)\in\hat{D}(\mu_{r})
and
l\in\{0,...,r\}
by Definition 5.11 since
\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}\in H_{t}
for
(t,s,\tau)\in\hat{D}(\mu_{r})
. Let

\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}:\hat{D}(\mu_{r})\mapsto\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(t,\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

and

G:(\mathbb{C}^{-})^{r+1}\to\mathbb{C},(w_{0},...,w_{r})\mapsto\prod_{j=0}^{r}(w_{j})^{q_{j}}.

Note that
G
is globally subanalytic. We obtain

\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}(t,s,\tau)=G(\sigma_{0}z_{0}^{*}(t,s,\tau),...,\sigma_{r}z_{r}^{*}(t,s,\tau))

for every
(t,s,\tau)\in\hat{D}(\mu_{r})
. Therefore
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}^{\otimes q}_{\mu_{r}}
is free-regular in
s
by Remark 5.66(2).

Let

d:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\min\{|{\mu_{r}(t)-\mu_{j}(t)}|\mid j\in\{0,...,r-1\}\bigl\}.

Note that
d
is regular (by Example 5.56(1) and Remark 5.55(2)) and
r
-persistent (by Example 5.43 and Remark 5.42(3)). Choose
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t\mapsto\min\{T(t),d(t)\}
. Let
D:=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{0,r},M)
. The following claim finishes the proof of Corollary 5.69.

Claim

The function
\mathcal{Z}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}=\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}|_{D(\mu_{r})}
is bounded in
\tau
.

Proof of the claim

Assume the contrary. Then there is
(t,s)\in\pi^{+}(D(\mu_{\kappa}))
and a definable curve
\gamma_{\tau}:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[
such that
(t,s,\gamma_{\tau}(y))\in D(\mu_{\kappa})
for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
and

\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{\mathcal{Z}_{\mu_{r}}^{\otimes q}(t,s,\gamma_{\tau}(y))}|=\lim_{y\searrow 0}|{(\sigma\mathcal{Z})^{\otimes q}(t,\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\gamma_{\tau}(y)})}|=\infty.

Note that
0<s<d
. Consider
\gamma:\textnormal{}]0,1[\textnormal{}\to H,y\mapsto(\gamma_{t}(y),\gamma_{z}(y))
with
\gamma_{t}(y)=t
and
\gamma_{z}(y)=\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\gamma_{\tau}(y)}
for
y\in\textnormal{}]0,1[
. Note that this curve is definable and compatible with
C
. Since
0<s<d
we have

\lim_{y\searrow 0}\gamma_{z}(y)-\mu_{l}(t)=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\mu_{r}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{r}\sigma_{j})se^{i\gamma_{\tau}(y)}-\mu_{l}(t)\neq 0

for
l\in\{0,...,r\}
which is a contradiction to Corollary 5.31(1).
\blacksquare

Now we investigate the case ”
\kappa=k
”: So for the rest of Section 5.1.3 we suppose
k\geq 0
. It is left to show that we can find a regular
k
-persistent
M:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and a regular
k
-soft
N:\pi(C)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

z_{l}^{*}:\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{0,k},N,M)(\mu_{k})\to\mathbb{C},(t,s,\tau)\mapsto z_{l}(t,\mu_{k}(t)+(\prod_{j=0}^{k}\sigma_{j})se^{i\tau}),

is free-regular in
s
for
l\in\{k,...,r\}
.

We start our considerations with the following three technical results.

5.70 Proposition

Let
D\subset H
be definable such that
D_{t}
is connected and
\mathbb{R}\cap D_{t}\neq\emptyset
for

t\in\pi(D)
. Let
f,g:D\to\mathbb{C}^{-}
be functions with the following properties.

(1)

f
and
g
are continuous in
z
.

(2)

We have
f(t,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
if
(t,z)\in D\cap(\pi(C)\times\mathbb{R})
.

(3)

f(t,z)g(t,z)\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
for every
(t,z)\in D
.

Then for every
(t,z)\in D

\log(f(t,z)g(t,z))=\log(f(t,z))+\log(g(t,z)).

Proof

Suppose that there is
(t,z^{*})\in D
such that

\log(f(t,z^{*})g(t,z^{*}))=\log(f(t,z^{*}))+\log(g(t,z^{*}))\pm 2\pi i

(and therefore

|{\textnormal{arg}(f(t,z^{*}))+\textnormal{arg}(g(t,z^{*}))}|\geq\pi.)

Note that

D_{t}\to\mathbb{R},z\mapsto\textnormal{arg}(f(t,z))+\textnormal{arg}(g(t,z)),

is continuous and that
|{\textnormal{arg}(f(t,z))+\textnormal{arg}(g(t,z))}|<\pi
if
z\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in\pi(C)
. By the intermediate value theorem (by taking a definable curve which connects
z^{*}
and a point
a\in D_{t}\cap\mathbb{R}
) there is
z^{\prime}\in D_{t}
such that

|{\textnormal{arg}(f(t,z^{\prime}))+\textnormal{arg}(g(t,z^{\prime}))}|=\pi.

This gives
\textnormal{arg}(f(t,z^{\prime})g(t,z^{\prime}))=\pi
. But then
f(t,z^{\prime})g(t,z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}
, a contradiction.
\blacksquare

5.71 Remark

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
D:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}\mid|{z}|<1/\mathrm{exp}_{m}(1)\}
and

L_{m}:D\to\mathbb{C},z\mapsto\log_{m}(-\log(z)).

Then
L_{m}
is well-defined , definable and holomorphic. We have
\log_{m}(-\log(z))\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
and
\log_{m}(-\log(z))\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
if
z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for
z\in D
.

Proof

Let
z\in D
. If
z\notin\mathbb{R}_{>0}
then

\log(z)=\log(|{z}|)+i\mathrm{arg}(z)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}

since
\mathrm{arg}(z)\neq 0
and therefore
\log_{m}(-\log(z))\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
. So assume
z\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We obtain

\mathrm{exp}_{m-1}(1)<-\log(z)
and therefore

\mathrm{exp}_{m-1-l}(1)<\log_{l}(-\log(z))

for every
l\in\{1,...,m-1\}
(where
\mathrm{exp}_{0}:=1
). We obtain
\log_{m}(-\log(z))>0
.

\blacksquare

5.72 Proposition

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

c:=\frac{1}{\mathrm{exp}_{m}\bigl(2\sqrt{\log^{2}(3/2)+\pi^{2}}\bigl)}

and
D:=\textnormal{}]0,c[\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[
. The function

T_{l}:D\to\mathbb{C}^{-},(s,\tau)\mapsto\log_{l}(-\log(se^{i\tau})),

is a well-defined globally subanalytic function in
\tau
with log-analytic support function for every
l\in\{0,...,m\}
.

Proof

Note that
se^{i\tau}\in\mathbb{C}^{-}
for every
(s,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\textnormal{}\times\textnormal{}]-\pi,\pi[
. So we obtain well-definability with Remark 5.71. Set

S_{0}:D\to\mathbb{C},(s,\tau)\mapsto\frac{i\tau}{\log(s)},

and inductively for
l\in\{1,...,m-1\}

S_{l}:D\to\mathbb{C},(s,\tau)\mapsto\frac{\log(1+S_{l-1}(s,\tau))}{\log_{l}(-\log(s))}.

Claim

Let
l\in\{0,...,m-1\}
. Then
S_{l}
is a well-defined globally subanalytic function in
\tau
with log-analytic support function. For every
(s,\tau)\in D
we have
|{S_{l}(s,\tau)}|<1/2
and

T_{l}=\log_{l}(-\log(s))(1+S_{l}(s,\tau)).

Proof of the claim

Let
(s,\tau)\in D
. We obtain

2\sqrt{\log^{2}(3/2)+\pi^{2}}<\log_{l}(-\log(s))\textnormal{ }(*)

for every
l\in\{0,...,m-1\}
. We do an induction on
l
. The case
l=0
is clear.

l-1\to l:
With the inductive hypothesis,
(*)
, and the definition of the complex logarithm we see that
S_{l}
is well-defined and that
|{S_{l}(s,\tau)}|<1/2
for every
(s,\tau)\in D
. We have for
(s,\tau)\in D

S_{l}(s,\tau)=\frac{\log^{*}(1+S_{l-1}(s,\tau))}{\log_{l}(-\log(s))}.

With the inductive hypothesis and Remark 1.25(2) it follows that
S_{l}
is a globally subanalytic function in
\tau
with log-analytic support function. Additionally we obtain for
(s,\tau)\in D
with the inductive hypothesis

\displaystyle\log_{l}(-\log(s)-i\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\log_{l-1}(-\log(s)-i\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\log_{l-1}(-\log(s))(1+S_{l-1}(s,\tau)))

\displaystyle=
